\section{Introduction}

This document describes the format of input files for the main components of the \SMSpp project.

\medskip
\noindent
Insomuch as the set of types of optimization problems (\Block in \SMSpp parlance) to be solved within the project, and the set of available solution methods for doing it (\Solver in \SMSpp parlance) may be expanding over time, this document is intended as dynamic: as new components will added, the document will grow to cover the corresponding input formats. Non-backward-compatible modification of existing input formats will be kept to a minimum.

\medskip
\noindent
The self-documenting nature of \netCDF, together with its portable readability and the schema definition of the various \Block, will make it possible to re-use all or some blocks of one \SMSpp-generated model in other contexts.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{General \code{netCDF} file structure}

\netCDF (network Common Data Form) \cite{netCDF} is a set of interfaces for array-oriented data access and a freely distributed collection of data access libraries for \code{C}, \code{Fortran}, \code{C++}, \code{Java}, and other languages. The \netCDF libraries support a machine-independent format for representing scientific data. Together, the interfaces, libraries, and format support the creation, access, and sharing of scientific data.

\medskip
\noindent
\netCDF data is:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item Self-Describing. A \netCDF file includes information about the data it contains.
 %
 \item Portable. A \netCDF file can be accessed by computers with different ways of storing integers, characters, and floating-point numbers.
 %
 \item Scalable. A small subset of a large dataset may be accessed efficiently.
 %
 \item Appendable. Data may be appended to a properly structured \netCDF file without copying the dataset or redefining its structure.
 %
 \item Sharable. One writer and multiple readers may simultaneously access the same \netCDF file.
 %
 \item Archivable. Access to all earlier forms of \netCDF data will be supported by current and future versions of the software.
\end{itemize}
%
For the purpose of this document, only a relatively small set of \netCDF concepts need be discussed.
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item \netCDF \textit{file}: is the basic container of \netCDF, e.g.~corresponding to a standard file in the filesystem of the host machine. In the \code{C++} interface used by \SMSpp it corresponds to an object of type \code{netCDF::NcFile}. A typical constructor of the object requires the pathname of the file (a standard \code{char *}) and the mode, which can be e.g.~\code{netCDF::NcFile::read} or \code{netCDF::NcFile::replace} for reading and writing, respectively.
 %
 \item \netCDF \group: a \netCDF file is organised into several \groups, which can be nested inside each other to an arbitrary degree. In the \code{C++} interface used by \SMSpp, each group corresponds to an object of type \code{netCDF::NcGroup}; note that \code{netCDF::NcFile} derives from \code{netCDF::NcGroup}, and therefore all capabilities of the group are shared by the file (but not vice-versa). Each \group has a unique string name by which it can retrieved; \code{netCDF::NcGroup} (and, therefore, \code{netCDF::NcFile}) has a method \code{getGroup()} taking as input a \code{std::string} with the group name and returning an \code{netCDF::NcGroup} object containing the \emph{child group} with the given name (if any) of that group.
 %
 \item \netCDF \attribute: is a simple data point specific of a netCDF group, having a unique string name by which it can retrieved and a simple value (\code{int}, \code{double}, \code{std::string}, \ldots). In the \code{C++} interface used by \SMSpp, it is represented by a \code{netCDF::NcGroupAtt} object, which can be obtained by its enclosing \code{netCDF::NcGroup} via the method \code{getAtt()} taking in input its name. Then, the method(s) \code{getValue()} (available with different signatures corresponding to the different supported basic data types) allows to retrieve its value.
 %
 \item \netCDF \dimension: it basically represent the different indices over which the actual data is indexed, each of which is given a name. In the \code{C++} interface used by \SMSpp, it is represented by a \code{netCDF::NcDim} object, which can be obtained by its enclosing \code{netCDF::NcGroup} via the method \code{getDim()} taking in input its name. The \dimension (a \code{size\_t} value) is returned by the method \code{getSize()}.
 %
 \item \netCDF \variable: this is the container for the ``complex'' data of a \groups. It has a name (\code{std::string}), a basic data type (\code{int}, \code{double}, \code{std::string}, \ldots) and in principle any number of \dimensions.  In the \code{C++} interface used by \SMSpp, it is represented by a \code{netCDF::NcVar} object, which can be obtained by its enclosing \code{netCDF::NcGroup} via the method \code{getVar()} taking in input its name. Once the \code{netCDF::NcVar} object is obtained, individual values of the \variable can be read by its method(s) \code{getVar()} (available with different signatures corresponding to the different supported basic data types), taking a \code{std::vector<size\_t>} as long as the number of \dimensions of the \variable (obtainable e.g.~with its method \code{NgetDimCount()}).
\end{itemize}
%
The above components allow to read and write structured data files with basically any nested format, which makes \netCDF ideal for representing the data characterizing \SMSpp, as discussed below.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%\newpage

\section{General \SMSpp file structure}

In \SMSpp, files can describe two different kinds of objects:
%
\begin{enumerate}
 \item \Block objects, representing the data characterizing a specific instance of a (structured) optimization problem to be solved;
 \item \Configuration objects, representing parameters of the solution process, either pertaining to a \Block (say, which of the different available formulations of the optimization problem should be used), or to a \Solver used to actually perform the optimization process.
\end{enumerate}
%
Both objects have \code{serialize()} and \code{deserialize()} methods allowing to save the current state to a given \netCDF \textit{file}/\group, as well as \emph{factories} allowing to create a new object from scratch out of a given \netCDF \textit{file}/\group.

\medskip
\noindent
The issue of \SMSpp is that an optimization problem is represented by a \Block, which can have any number of sub-\Block (recursively) representing parts of the problem with a specific structure. Yet, ``specific structure'' here may mean that the enclosing \Block can make some assumptions on what kind of sub-\Block it contains, but it may not know exactly their type. In \code{C++} parlance, \Block is an abstract base class, and specific optimization problems are represented by specific derived classes (\dBlock). A given \dBlock can make assumption on the type of its sub-\Block, but there can be many different variants of it, represented by different derived classes from the same base one. Thus, clearly the data-reading must be organized in such a way that each \dBlock is responsible for retrieving that which is necessary to it without making assumptions about the global organization of the file. This does not only apply to the data of the instance, but also to \emph{configuration} options that a \dBlock may have. In fact, several aspect of the \dBlock representing a given problem (which formulation is used, if and how variables and constraint of the formulation are dynamically generated, which parts of the primal/dual solution are saved, \ldots) can be implemented in different ways. Note that these decisions are in principle independent from the specific instance, and therefore are conceptually to be stored in, and retrieved from, a separate container than that holding the data of the \Block; this is why the \Configuration class is provided. Since \Block is a tree-shaped nested data structure, \Configuration also have to be such. Clearly, \netCDF perfectly suit this kind of requirement; in particular, the standing assumption for both classes is that
%
\begin{center}
 \emph{the data of one object} (\Block/\Configuration) \emph{is all to be found in the same \netCDF group; \\ the data of its ``sub-objects'' (sub-\Block/sub-\Configuration) is to be found in child groups of the group}.
\end{center}

\medskip
\noindent
Hence, the format of a \SMSpp \netCDF file depends on whether it holds \Block information, \Configuration information, or both. For the latter, there are actually two separate kind of \Configuration information that can be attached to a \dBlock. The first describes the above-mentioned options that are inherent to the \dBlock itself, which are described by the specific derived class \BlockConfig of \Configuration. However, a \dBlock need be solved, which is done by \Solver objects; each \Block can have an arbitrary number of \Solver ``registered'' with it. A \Solver can be an arbitrarily complex solution process; in particular, the structure of \Block immediately implies that one can register \Solver with any sub-\Block, which is actually useful e.g.~to decomposition algorithms. Hence, not only each \dSolver can have an arbitrarily large set of algorithmic configuration options; an arbitrarily large set of \Solver can be attached to a \Block and to all of its sub-\Block, recursively. Thus, the different derived class \BlockSolverConfig of \Configuration is provided to describe all this information. Note, again, that this is conceptually separate from both the specific instance encoded in the \dBlock and the choice of the \Block-specific options encoded in the \BlockConfig, which justifies why a separate container is used.

\medskip
\noindent
To allow holding all this different information, a \SMSpp \netCDF file has the following general structure. It must contain (in the \code{netCDF::NcFile} object, which is also a \code{netCDF::NcGroup}) an \attribute \cstr{SMS++\_file\_type} of \code{int} type which specifies which of three different kinds of file it is. The values are encoded in the \code{enum} \code{smspp\_netCDF\_file\_type} defined in \code{SMSTypedefs.h}, as follows:
%
\begin{itemize}
  \item \code{eProbFile = 0}: the \textit{file} (which is also a \group) has any number of child \groups with names \cstr{Prob\_0}, \cstr{Prob\_1}, \ldots In turn, each child \group has \emph{exactly three} child \groups with names \cstr{Block}, \cstr{BlockConfig} and \cstr{BlockSolver}, respectively. The first is intended to contain the serialization of a \dBlock, the second the serialization of a \dBlockConfig of the same \dBlock, and the third the serialization of a \dBlockSolverConfig of the same \dBlock, although any of the three can in principle be empty. If any of the child is not empty, it \emph{must} necessarily contain all the information necessary to reconstruct the specific instance.
  %
  \item \code{eBlockFile = 1}: the \textit{file} (which is also a \group) has any number of child \groups with names \cstr{Block\_0}, \cstr{Block\_1}, \ldots Each child \group contains the serialization of a \dBlock.
  %
  \item \code{eConfigFile = 2}: the \textit{file} (which is also a \group) has any number of child \groups with names \cstr{Config\_0}, \cstr{Config\_1}, \dots Each child \group contains the serialization of a \dConfiguration. Note that no distinction is required between a \dBlockConfig and a \dBlockSolverConfig, since they are both derived from \Configuration.
\end{itemize}
%
Note that (some variants of) the \code{deserialize()} methods of \Block / \Configuration allow to directly specify which of the different objects stored in the file to read via its index $i$ in the ``name''.

\medskip
\noindent
To completely describe the file formats we now have to describe the format of the \netCDF \group as far as the base \Block and \Configuration (for the latter, actually both sub-classes \BlockConfig and \BlockSolverConfig) are concerned, i.e., the (possibly, small) part of the contents that do not depend on the specific derived class.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{\Block description}\label{ssec:Block}

The only content that any \netCDF \group describing a \dBlock must necessarily have is a string \attribute with name \cstr{type} containing the classname of the object. This must be \emph{exactly} what is returned by the protected virtual method \code{classname()}, since it is used in the \emph{factory} when deserializing the object. Note that template classes derived from \Block are allowed, with the standard notation \cstr{class\_name<template\_parameters>}. However, due to a technical issue about using macros (see the comments to \code{SMSpp\_insert\_in\_factory\_cpp\_*} in \code{SMSTypedefs.h}), \emph{the name given to the class must not contain any ``,'' (comma)}. That is, something like ``\code{myBlock< std::pair< int , int > >}'' \emph{cannot} be used when defining the \Block. The typical way is to resort to a using declaration, i.e.,

\medskip
\begin{tabular}{l}
 \code{using myBlock\_i\_i = myBlock< std::pair< int , int > >;} \\
 \code{SMSpp\_insert\_in\_factory\_cpp\_1\_t( myBlock\_i\_i );}
\end{tabular}

\medskip
\noindent
This implies that the \cstr{type} of that \Block as returned by \code{classname()}, and therefore the one that has to be used in the \netCDF file, must necessarily be ``\cstr{myBlock\_i\_i}''. The exact string will typically be defined in the .cpp file where the \Block is implemented, precisely in the corresponding invocation of the macro \code{SMSpp\_insert\_in\_factory\_cpp\_*}. In all non-complicated-template cases, the name should just be the only possible form in which the classname can be written.

\medskip
Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} \attribute, all \Block support an optional  \cstr{name} \attribute of string type. If present, this is supposed to  contain the ``name'' of the \Block, as returned by the \code{name()} method; if not present (and not set in-memory via \code{set\_name()}), \code{name()} will return an empty string. This is not a classname, but rather a string describing something about the \Block that is supposedly useful when printing/debugging it.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{\Configuration description}

The only content that any \netCDF \group describing a \dConfiguration must necessarily have is a string \attribute with name \cstr{type} containing the classname of the object. This must be exactly what is returned by the protected virtual method \code{classname()}, since it is used in the \emph{factory} when deserializing the object. Note that template classes derived from \Configuration are allowed, with the standard notation \cstr{class\_name<template\_parameters>}; see for instance the template \code{SimpleConfiguration<>} class. However, the same issue with ``,'' (commas) not being allowed to the class name as in \Block applies here, see \S \ref{ssec:Block} for more details.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsection{\BlockConfig description}\label{sec:BlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \BlockConfig can contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item the \attribute \cstr{diff} of \code{netCDF::NcInt} type telling if the
  information in it has to be taken as ``the configuration to be set''
  ($\cstr{diff} \le 0$) or as ``the changes to be made from the current
  configuration'' ($\cstr{diff} > 0$); this attribute is optional: if it is
  not provided, then $\cstr{diff} = 1$ is assumed;

\item the \group \cstr{static\_constraints} containing a \Configuration object
  for the static constraints of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{dynamic\_constraints} containing a \Configuration
  object for the dynamic constraints of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{static\_variables} containing a \Configuration object
  for the static variables of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{dynamic\_variables} containing a \Configuration object
  for the dynamic variables of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{objective} containing a \Configuration object for the
  objective of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{is\_feasible} containing a \Configuration object for
  the \code{is\_feasible()} method of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{is\_optimal} containing a \Configuration object for the
  \code{is\_optimal()} method of the \Block;
 %
\item the \group \cstr{solution} containing a \Configuration object for the
  methods of the \Block dealing with solutions (\code{get\_Solution()} and
  \code{map\_[back/forward]\_solution()});
 %
\item the \group \cstr{extra} containing a \Configuration object, which has no
  direct use in the base \Block class, but is added so that derived classes
  can put there any configuration information without having to define further
  derived classes form \BlockConfig (which, however, they can still do if they
  want).
\end{itemize}

All these \groups are optional. If a \group is not provided, the corresponding
field of the class is filled with a \code{nullptr}, indicating that the
``default'' configuration (whatever that may mean for the \dBlock in question)
has to be used.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\RBlockConfig description}\label{sec:RBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \RBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):

\item the \dimension \cstr{n\_sub\_Block} containing the number of
  \BlockConfig descriptions for the sub-\Block of the current \Block; this
  \dimension is optional; if it is not provided, then $\cstr{n\_sub\_Block} =
  0$ is assumed.

\item With $n$ being the size of \cstr{n\_sub\_Block}, $n$ \groups, with name
  \cstr{sub-BlockConfig\_$i$} for all $i = 0, \ldots, n - 1$, containing each
  the description of a \BlockConfig for one of the sub-\Block of the current
  \dBlock. Each of these \groups is optional. If a \group is absent then the
  pointer to the \BlockConfig for the corresponding sub-\Block is considered
  to be a \code{nullptr} (default configuration).

\item With $n$ being the size of \code{n\_sub\_Block}, a one-dimensional
  \variable with name \cstr{sub-Block-id}, of size $n$ and type
  \code{netCDF::NcString}, containing the identification of the sub-\Block
  such that \code{sub-BlockConfig\_$i$} contains the \BlockConfig for the
  sub-\Block whose identification is \code{sub-Block-id[ $i$ ]} for all $i =
  0, \ldots, n - 1$. The identification of the sub-\Block can be either its
  name (see \code{Block::name()}) or its index in the list of sub-\Block of
  its father \Block. This \variable is optional. If it is not provided, then
  the $i$-th \BlockConfig is associated with the $i$-th sub-\Block of the
  \Block for all $i = 0, \ldots, n - 1$ (i.e., $i$ is taken as the index of
  the sub-\Block and \cstr{sub-Block-id[ $i$ ]} is assumed to be $i$).

  \note{If the name of the \Block is used as its identification, then
      the first character of this name cannot be a digit.}
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\CBlockConfig description}\label{sec:CBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \CBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):

\item the \dimension \cstr{n\_Config\_Constraint} containing the number of
  \ComputeConfig descriptions associated with the \Constraint of the current
  \Block; this \dimension is optional; if it is not provided, then
  $\cstr{n\_Config\_Constraint} = 0$ is assumed.

\item with $p$ being the size of \cstr{n\_Config\_Constraint}, a
  one-dimensional \variable called \cstr{Constraint\_group\_id}, of size $p$
  and type \code{netCDF::NcString}, containing the identification of the group
  of \Constraints that require a \ComputeConfig. The $i$-th element of this
  vector, \code{Constraint\_group\_id[ $i$ ]}, is the identification of the
  group to which the $i$-th \Constraint belongs. For each $i = 0, \ldots, p -
  1$, \code{Constraint\_group\_id[ $i$ ]} can be indicated in two ways: it is
  either (i) the name of the group of \Constraint (see
  \code{Block::get\_s\_const\_name()} and \code{Block::get\_d\_const\_name()})
  or the index of the group as defined in \code{Block::ConstraintID}.

  \note{If a \Constraint group is being identified using the name of the group
    (rather than the index of the group), then
    \begin{itemize}
    \item the first character of this name cannot be a digit;
    \item the static group has priority over the dynamic group of \Constraint:
      if there is a group of static \Constraint with the given name, then this
      group is considered. Otherwise, the group of dynamic \Constraint with
      that name is considered.
  \end{itemize}}

  This \variable is mandatory if $\cstr{n\_Config\_Constraint} > 0$.

\item with $p$ being the size of \cstr{n\_Config\_Constraint}, a
  one-dimensional \variable called \cstr{Constraint\_index}, of size $p$ and
  type \code{netCDF::NcUint}, containing the index of the \Constraint that
  require a \ComputeConfig. The $i$-th element of this vector,
  \code{Constraint\_index[ $i$ ]}, is the index of the $i$-th \Constraint
  (which belongs to the group indicated by \code{Constraint\_group\_id[ $i$
  ]}). See \code{Block::ConstraintID} for the definition of the index of a
  \Constraint in a group. This \variable is optional. If it is not provided,
  then $\code{Constraint\_index[ $i$ ]} = i$ for all $i = 0, \ldots, p - 1$ is
  assumed.

\item $p$ \groups, with name \code{Config\_Constraint\_$i$} for all $i = 0,
  \ldots, p - 1$, containing each the description of a \ComputeConfig
  associated with the $i$-th \Constraint indicated by the pair (
  \code{Constraint\_group\_id[ $i$ ]}, \code{Constraint\_index[ $i$ ]} );
  these \groups are optional; if \code{Config\_Constraint\_$i$} is not
  provided, then \code{nullptr} (default configuration) is assumed for the
  $i$-th \Constraint.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\OBlockConfig description}\label{sec:OBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \OBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):

\item a \group with name \cstr{Config\_Objective}, containing the description
  of a \ComputeConfig associated with the \Objective of the current \Block;
  this \group is optional; if it is not provided, then \code{nullptr} (default
  configuration) is assumed.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\OCBlockConfig description}\label{sec:OCBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \OCBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \OBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:OBlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \CBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:CBlockConfig}).
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\ORBlockConfig description}\label{sec:ORBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \ORBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \OBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:OBlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \RBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:RBlockConfig}).
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\CRBlockConfig description}\label{sec:CRBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \CRBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \CBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:CBlockConfig}):
\item all that is needed to describe a \RBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:RBlockConfig}).
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\OCRBlockConfig description}\label{sec:OCRBlockConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \OCRBlockConfig should also contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item all that is needed to describe a \BlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:BlockConfig});

\item all that is needed to describe a \OBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:OBlockConfig});

\item all that is needed to describe a \CBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:CBlockConfig}):

\item all that is needed to describe a \RBlockConfig
  (see~\ref{sec:RBlockConfig}).
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsection{\BlockSolverConfig description}\label{sec:BlockSolverConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \BlockSolverConfig can contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}

\item the \attribute \cstr{diff} of \code{netCDF::NcInt} type telling if the
  information in it has to be taken as ``the configuration to be set''
  ($\cstr{diff} \le 0$) or as ``the changes to be made from the current
  configuration'' ($\cstr{diff} > 0$); this attribute is optional, if it is
  not provided, then $\cstr{diff} = 0$ is assumed;
%
\item the \dimension \cstr{n\_SolverConfig} containing the number of \Solver
  that are to be attached to the \Block, and therefore the number of their
  \code{SolverConfig} objects; this dimension is optional, if it is not
  provided, then $\cstr{n\_SolverConfig} = 0$ is considered;
%
\item the \variable \cstr{SolverNames}, of type \code{netCDF::NcString} and
  indexed over the \dimension \cstr{n\_SolverConfig}; the $i$-th entry of the
  variable is assumed to contain the classname of a \dSolver object to be
  attached to the \Block (this must be exact, i.e., exactly as returned by the
  protected virtual method \code{Solver::classname()}, since it is used in the
  \emph{factory} when creating the object; this variable is mandatory if
  $\cstr{n\_SolverConfig} > 0$;
%
\item with $n$ being the size of \code{n\_SolverConfig}, $n$ \groups, with
  name \cstr{SolverConfig\_$i$} for all $i = 0, \ldots, n - 1$, containing
  each the description of a \ComputeConfig object for the $i$-th \dSolver;
  these groups are optional; if \cstr{SolverConfig\_$i$} is not provided, then
  \code{nullptr} is considered for the $i$-th \ComputeConfig.
\end{itemize}
%

\medskip
\noindent
Note that the \dConfiguration objects to be found in the \groups
\cstr{SolverConfig\_$i$} are assumed to be of the specific type
\ComputeConfig, whose format is described in~\ref{sec:ComputeConfig}.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsubsection{\RBlockSolverConfig description}\label{sec:RBlockSolverConfig}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, and the
\dimensions, \variables, and \groups of a \BlockSolverConfig, a \SMSpp \netCDF
\group for a \RBlockSolverConfig can contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
\item the \dimension \cstr{n\_BlockSolverConfig} containing the number of
  \BlockSolverConfig descriptions for the sub-\Block of the current \Block; it
  is optional; if it is not provided, then we assume
  $\cstr{n\_BlockSolverConfig} = 0$.

\item with $m$ being the size of \cstr{n\_BlockSolverConfig}, $m$ \groups,
  with name \cstr{BlockSolverConfig\_$i$} for all $i = 0, \ldots, m - 1$,
  containing each the description of a \BlockSolverConfig for one of the
  sub-\Block of the current \Block. If some group
  \cstr{BlockSolverConfig\_$i$} does not exist, \code{nullptr} is used;

\item With $n$ being the size of \cstr{n\_BlockSolverConfig}, a
  one-dimensional \variable with name \cstr{sub-Block-id}, of size $n$ and
  type \code{netCDF::NcString}, containing the identification of the
  sub-\Block such that \code{BlockSolverConfig\_$i$} contains the
  \BlockSolverConfig for the sub-\Block whose identification is
  \code{sub-Block-id[ $i$ ]} for all $i = 0, \ldots, n - 1$. The
  identification of the sub-\Block can be either its name (see
  \code{Block::name()}) or its index in the list of sub-\Block of its father
  \Block. This \variable is optional. If it is not provided, then the $i$-th
  \BlockSolverConfig is associated with the $i$-th sub-\Block of the \Block
  for all $i = 0, \ldots, n - 1$ (i.e., $i$ is taken as the index of the
  sub-\Block and \code{sub-Block-id[ $i$ ]} is assumed to be $i$).

  \note{If the name of the \Block is used as its identification, then
    the first character of this name cannot be a digit.}

  The individual \groups \cstr{BlockSolverConfig\_$i$} are optional. If
  \cstr{BlockSolverConfig\_$i$} is not provided, then \code{nullptr} is
  considered. Note that the size of the vector of sub-\BlockSolverConfig is
  allowed to be different than the number of sub-\Block.
\end{itemize}


%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsection{\ComputeConfig description}\label{sec:ComputeConfig}

\ComputeConfig (defined in \code{ThinComputeInterface.h}) is a class of \dConfiguration objects specifically tailored for representing sets of algorithmic parameters of solution algorithms, like the ones presumably required by a \dSolver.
Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dConfiguration, a \SMSpp \netCDF \group for a \ComputeConfig can contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item the \attribute \cstr{diff} of \code{int} type containing the value for the \code{f\_diff} field of the \ComputeConfig (basically, a bool telling if the information in it has to be taken as ``the configuration to be set'' or as ``the changes to be made from the current configuration'');
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{num\_int\_par} containing the number of \code{int} parameters;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{int\_par\_names}, of type string and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_int\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the string name of an \code{int} parameter;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{int\_par\_vals}, of type \code{int} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_int\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the value of the \code{int} parameter whose string name is to be found in the $i$-th entry of \cstr{int\_par\_names};
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{num\_dbl\_par} containing the number of \code{double} parameters;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{dbl\_par\_names}, of type string and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_dbl\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the string name of a \code{double} parameter;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{dbl\_par\_vals}, of type \code{double} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_dbl\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the value of the \code{double} parameter whose string name is to be found in the $i$-th entry of \cstr{dbl\_par\_names};
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{num\_str\_par} containing the number of string parameters;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{str\_par\_names}, of type string and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_str\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the string name of a string parameter;
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{str\_par\_vals}, of type string and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{num\_str\_par}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the value of the string parameter whose string name is to be found in the $i$-th entry of \cstr{str\_par\_names};
 %
 \item the \group \cstr{extra} containing a \Configuration object, which has no direct use in the base \ComputeConfig class, but is added so that derived classes can put there any configuration information without having to define further derived classes form \ComputeConfig (which, however, they can still do if they want).
\end{itemize}
%
The three \dimensions \cstr{num\_*\_par} are mandatory. If any of these is zero, the corresponding variables \cstr{*\_par\_names} and \cstr{*\_par\_vals} may not be defined. The \cstr{extra} \group may not exist, in which case the corresponding \Configuration object is set to a \code{nullptr}.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Auxiliary structures formats}

This section presents the description of some auxiliary structures that are
necessary to describe some \dBlock. The \code{AbstractPath}, presented in
\S \ref{sec:AbstractPath}, is used to specify a path from a \Block to
some \SMSpp element (\Block, \Constraint, \Variable, \Objective, or \Function)
defined in that \Block or in any of their nested \Block, recursively.  The
\code{SimpleDataMapping}, presented in \S \ref{sec:SimpleDataMapping},
offers a mechanism for changing the data of a \Block.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsection{\code{AbstractPath}}\label{sec:AbstractPath}

The \code{AbstractPath} represents a path from a \Block, here referred to as
the reference \Block, to one of its elements (the target element): a \Block, a
\code{Constraint}, a \code{Variable}, an \code{Objective}, or a
\code{Function}. The target element can directly belong to the reference
\Block itself (even be the reference \Block itself) or belong to any of its
sons, recursively. The reference \Block is not explicitly represented in the
path. In fact, the representation of the path is independent from the
reference \Block. For the path to be meaningful, the reference \Block should
be clear from the context. The reference \Block must be available when the
path is constructed and when the target element is retrieved. Furthermore, the
type of the target element cannot always be inferred from the path. The type
of the target element, therefore, must also be known from the context.

The \code{AbstractPath} is particularly useful for the serialization and
deserialization of pointers to objects. If an object stores pointers to other
objects (for example, a \code{Function} has a set of pointers to
\code{Variable}, others have pointers to \Block), these pointers cannot be
serialized and deserialized as such. Rather, an ``abstract representation'' of
these pointers has to be serialized, from which the pointers can be
reconstructed at deserialization time; this is the facility that
\code{AbstractPath} offers. The fact that the representation of the path is
independent from the reference \Block facilitates its serialization and
deserialization, and makes it possible to use the same path to target
different objects (say, two \code{Constraint} ``in the same position'' in two
``twin'' \Block can be represented by the same \code{AbstractPath}).

The path is defined as a sequence of nodes. Each node has one of the following
types:

\begin{itemize}
 \item `O', if the node is associated with an \Objective;

 \item `B', if the node is associated with a \Block;

 \item `C', if the node is associated with a static \Constraint;

 \item `c', if the node is associated with a dynamic \Constraint;

 \item `V', if the node is associated with a static \Variable;

 \item `v', if the node is associated with a dynamic \Variable.
\end{itemize}

Notice that, for \Constraint and \Variable, an upper case letter indicates that
the element is static, while a lower case letter indicates that the element is
dynamic. Also notice that there is no node associated with a \Function, but
this does not prevent one from constructing a path to a \Function. Although the
nodes in the path are stored in forward order, i.e., the first node is the
origin of the path and the last one is the destination, it is easier to
understand the path if we look at it backwards.

Consider the path from some reference \Block to some \Variable. The last node
in this path necessarily has the `V' or `v' type, indicating this is a path to
a \Variable. This \Variable belongs to some \Block and it is either static or
dynamic. This last node has all information needed to retrieve this \Variable
from its father \Block: an indication of whether the \Variable is static or
dynamic, the index of the group to which it belongs, and the index of the
\Variable within that group.

\note{The index of a \Variable (or \Constraint) within a group is a single
  number and may not be entirely obvious which number it should be (specially
  for multidimensional arrays and (multidimensional) arrays of lists). Please
  refer to the \S \ref{sec:single_AbstractPath} for an explanation of
  the index of a \Variable (or \Constraint) within a group.}

A `V' or `v' node is always preceded by a `B' node, unless it is the only node
in the path. If the path has only a single node, which is a `V' or `v' node,
then the \Variable this path refers to is defined in the reference \Block. In
other words, if the target \Variable of the path belongs to the reference
\Block, then the path is formed by a single node whose type is either `V' or
`v'.

A `B' node is associated with a \Block and may contain the index of this
\Block in the vector of nested \Blocks of its father \Block. There is one case
in which this node does not have this index, which is when the node is
associated with the reference \Block. Since the reference \Block is the root
of the three that contains the path, no allusion to the father of the
reference \Block must be made. In this case, the index has the value
$+\infty$. If the index of a `B' node is not $+\infty$, then this node is
necessarily preceded by another `B' node, which is associated with the father
of that \Block.

An `O' node, which is associated with an \Objective, is either preceded by a
`B' node (which is associated with the \Block that owns that \Objective) or is
the only node in the path. If it is the last node in the path, then the target
element is either this \Objective or the \Function in that \Objective (in
which case that \Objective is an \code{FRealObjective}). The type of the
target element must be known from the context. If this is not the last node in
the path, then the type of the next node in the path is `B' and it is
associated with the inner \Block of either a \code{BendersBFunction} or a
\code{LagBFunction} which is the \Function of that \Objective (and thus that
\Objective is actually an \code{FRealObjective}).

Finally, a `C' or `c' node, which is associated with a \Constraint, has
characteristics pertaining both the `V' (and `v') and the `O' nodes. Like the
`V' (and `v') node, it has an indication of whether it is static or not, the
index of the group to which it belongs, and the index of the \Constraint
within that group (exactly as defined for \Variable). Like an `O' node, a `C'
or `c' node is either preceded by a `B' node (which is associated with the
\Block that owns that \Constraint) or is the only node in the path. If it is
the last node in the path, then the target element is either this \Constraint
or the \Function in that \Constraint (in which case that \Constraint is an
\code{FRowConstraint}). The type of the target element must be known from the
context. If this is not the last node in the path, then the type of the next
node in the path is `B' and it is associated with the inner \Block of either a
\code{BendersBFunction} or a \code{LagBFunction} which is the \Function of
that \Constraint (and thus that \Constraint is actually an
\code{FRowConstraint}).

Besides the description of an \code{AbstractPath}, we also provide a
description of a vector of \code{AbstractPath}. In
\S \ref{sec:single_AbstractPath} we present the description of a single
\code{AbstractPath} and in \S \ref{sec:vector_AbstractPath} we present
the description of a vector of \code{AbstractPath}.

\subsubsubsection{A single \code{AbstractPath}}\label{sec:single_AbstractPath}

\noindent
An \code{AbstractPath} is described by the following \netCDF \variables and
\dimensions. These do not need to be in a specific \netCDF \group, unless of
course one has to specify more than one \code{AbstractPath} in a \group, in
which case it is necessary to make specific \groups because the name of a
\variable/\dimension cannot be duplicated in a given \group.

\begin{itemize}
\item the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength} containing the number $N$ of nodes in the path;

\item the \variable \cstr{PathNodeTypes}, of type \code{netCDF::NcChar} and
  indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength}, contains the types of the
  nodes in the path;

\item the \variable \cstr{PathGroupIndices}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength}, contains the group
  indices associated with each node in the path;

\item the \variable \cstr{PathElementIndices}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength}, contains the element
  indices associated with each node in the path. This \variable is
  optional. If it is not provided, then \code{PathElementIndices[i]} is
  assumed to be \code{inf} for all $i \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{TotalLength}-1\}$.
\end{itemize}

\note{All indices mentioned here belong to zero-based numbered sequences, that
  is, sequences whose first element is 0.}

The $i$-th element in each of these arrays is associated with the $i$-th node
in the path, for $i \in \{0, \dots, N-1\}$. The type of a node may be indicated
by any of the following letters:

\begin{itemize}
\item `O', if the node is associated with an \Objective;

\item `B', if the node is associated with a \Block;

\item `C', if the node is associated with a static \Constraint;

\item `c', if the node is associated with a dynamic \Constraint;

\item `V', if the node is associated with a static \Variable;

\item `v', if the node is associated with a dynamic \Variable.
\end{itemize}

Notice that, for \Constraint and \Variable, an upper case letter indicates
that the element is static, while a lower case letter indicates that the
element is dynamic.

For a node whose type is `O', i.e., representing a node associated with an
\Objective, the values stored in the variables \cstr{PathGroupIndices} and
\cstr{PathElementIndices} are meaningless. If it is the last node in the path,
then the path refers to an \Objective. Otherwise, the next node in the path
must be of type `B'.

For node whose type is `B', i.e., representing a node associated with a \Block
$B$, the values stored in the variable \cstr{PathElementIndices} are
meaningless. If this is the $i$-th node in the path with $i < N - 1$, i.e., it
is not the last node of the path, the value stored in
\code{PathGroupIndices[i]} is the index of the sub-\Block of \Block $B$ which
is the next node in the path. If $i = N-1$, i.e., it is the last node in the
path, then the destination of the path is a \Block which must be

\begin{itemize}
\item the \Block $B$ itself if $\code{PathGroupIndices[i]} = +\infty$;

\item the $j$-th sub-\Block of \Block $B$, where $j = \code{PathGroupIndices[i]}$.
\end{itemize}

If the $i$-th node has type `C' or `c', representing a \Constraint, or `V' or
`v', representing a \Variable, the variable \code{PathGroupIndices[i]} stores
the index of the (static or dynamic) group to which the element belongs. The
variable \code{PathElementIndices[i]} stores the index of the element in that
group.

A static group can be one of three types:

\begin{enumerate}
\item It is a single \Constraint or \Variable;

\item It is a vector of \Constraint or \Variable;

\item It is a multidimensional array of \Constraint or \Variable.
\end{enumerate}

In the first case, in which the group is a single \Constraint or \Variable,
the value of \code{PathElementIndices[i]} is 0. In the second case, in which
the group is a vector of \Constraint or \Variable,
\code{PathElementIndices[i]} is the index of the element in that vector. In
the last case, in which the group is a multidimensional array of \Constraint
or \Variable, \code{PathElementIndices[i]} is the index of the element in the
vectorized multidimensional array in row-major layout. For instance, if the
multidimensional array has two dimensions with sizes $m$ and $n$,
respectively, then the element at position $(p, q)$ would have an element
index equal to $n p + q$ (recall the indices start from 0). In general, for a
multidimensional array with $k$ dimensions with sizes $(n_0, \dots, n_{k-1})$,
the element at position $(i_0, \dots, i_{k-1})$ would have an element index
equal to
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{r = 0}^{k-1} \left( \prod_{s = r + 1}^{k-1} n_s \right) i_r.
  \label{eq:element_index}
\end{equation}

A dynamic group can be one of three types:

\begin{enumerate}
\item It is list of \Constraint or \Variable;

\item It is a vector of lists of \Constraint or \Variable;

\item It is a multidimensional array of lists of \Constraint or \Variable.
\end{enumerate}

In the first case, in which the group is a list of \Constraint or \Variable,
\code{PathElementIndices[i]} is the index of the element in that list. In the
second case, in which the group is a vector of lists of \Constraint or
\Variable, for an element at position $j$ of the $k$-th list of the vector,
\code{PathElementIndices[i]} is given by
\[
   j + \sum_{t = 0}^{k-1} s_t
\]
where $s_t$ is the number of elements in the $t$-th list of the vector. The
last case is analogous.

\subsubsubsection{Vector of \code{AbstractPath}}\label{sec:vector_AbstractPath}

\noindent
A vector of \code{AbstractPath} is described by the following \netCDF
\variables and \dimensions. These do not need to be in a specific \netCDF
\group, unless of course one has to specify more than one vector of
\code{AbstractPath} in a \group, in which case it is necessary to make specific
\groups because the name of a \variable/\dimension cannot be duplicated in a
given \group.

\begin{itemize}
\item the \dimension \cstr{PathDim};

\item the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength};

\item the \variable \cstr{PathStart}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint} and
  indexed over \cstr{PathDim};

\item the \variable \cstr{PathNodeTypes}, of type \code{netCDF::NcChar} and
  indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength};

\item the \variable \cstr{PathGroupIndices}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength};

\item the \variable \cstr{PathElementIndices}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TotalLength}. This \variable is
  optional. If it is not provided, then \code{PathElementIndices[i]} is
  assumed to be \code{inf} for all $i \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{TotalLength}-1\}$.
\end{itemize}

The format of a vector of \code{AbstractPath} is similar to that of a single
\code{AbstractPath} with a few differences. Firstly, there is a \dimension
called \cstr{PathDim} which contains the number of paths that are described in
that group. Secondly, there is a one-dimensional \variable called
\cstr{PathStart}, indexed over \cstr{PathDim}, which contains the index where
the description of each \code{AbstractPath} starts. Then there are the
one-dimensional \variables \cstr{PathNodeTypes}, \cstr{PathGroupIndices}, and
\cstr{PathElementIndices}, which store the types of nodes in the paths, the
group indices, and the element indices, respectively. The description of the
$k$-th \code{AbstractPath}, for $k < \code{PathDim} - 1$ is given in those
arrays between the indices \code{PathStart[k]} and \code{PathStart[k+1]},
i.e., in
\[
( \code{PathNodeTypes[ PathStart[ k ] ]} , \dots ,
  \code{PathNodeTypes[ PathStart[ k+1 ] - 1 ]} ),
\]
\[
( \code{PathGroupIndices[ PathStart[ k ] ]} , \dots ,
  \code{PathGroupIndices[ PathStart[ k+1 ] - 1 ]} )
\]
and
\[
( \code{PathElementIndices[ PathStart[ k ] ]} , \dots ,
  \code{PathElementIndices[ PathStart[ k+1 ] - 1 ]} ).
\]

The description of the last path is given between the indices
\code{PathStart[PathDim - 1]} and \code{TotalLength - 1}, where
\cstr{TotalLength} is a \dimension containing the size of the arrays
\cstr{PathNodeTypes}, \cstr{PathGroupIndices}, and \cstr{PathElementIndices}
(and, therefore, these arrays are indexed over \cstr{TotalLength}).

The paths are represented in the same format as specified for a single
\code{AbstractPath}, except that here they are concatenated in the arrays
\cstr{PathNodeTypes}, \cstr{PathGroupIndices}, and \cstr{PathElementIndices}.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsubsection{\code{SimpleDataMapping}}\label{sec:SimpleDataMapping}

\code{DataMapping} defines an interface for all types of data mappings. The
idea of a data mapping is to allow, in particular, to map the values given by
a vector of \code{double} into the data of some object. Among its functions,
it has the \code{set\_data()} pair of functions, which have the following
signature:

\begin{verbatim}
    virtual void set_data( const std::vector< double > & data ,
                           c_ModParam issueMod = eModBlck ,
                           c_ModParam issueAMod = eModBlck ) const;

    virtual void set_data( const Eigen::ArrayXd & data ,
                           c_ModParam issueMod = eModBlck ,
                           c_ModParam issueAMod = eModBlck ) const;
\end{verbatim}
The idea of these functions is that the values of some data of an object can
be modified considering the given \code{data} parameter. Typically, a
\code{DataMapping} could be used to set the data of a \Block. In this case, a
pointer to that \Block must be available. Pointers to a \Block can be
serialized and deserialized considering its \code{AbstractPath}, which is
relative to some reference \Block.

\code{SimpleDataMapping} is a template class that derives from
\code{DataMapping} and is used to define some common kinds of data mapping. We
define two vectors: the ``large'' one and the ``small'' one. The ``large''
vector refers to the vector that is given as input to the \code{set\_data()}
method. This is the vector containing all the data that can be used by the
\code{SimpleDataMapping}. The ``small'' vector is a vector formed by a subset
of the elements of the ``large'' vector. This is the vector that will
effectively be used to perform some computation. This computation is typically
the task of changing the data of some object based on this ``small''
vector. There is a mapping defined by the \code{SetFrom} set that specifies
which elements of the ``large'' vector are used to compose the ``small''
vector. This \code{SetFrom} set contains the indices of these elements in the
``large'' vector. The ``small'' vector is the one that will typically impact
the data of some object. The \code{SetTo} set can be used to specify which
part of this data is affected. Actually, \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} are
ordered multisets, but we will refer to them as sets for simplicity.

As an example, consider the case in which the ``large'' vector contains data
related to costs and capacities of arcs of a network. Suppose this network is
represented by a fictitious class \code{Network}. A \code{SimpleDataMapping}
could be used to set the capacities of the arcs of a \code{Network} object
considering the data provided by this ``large'' vector. Some elements of this
``large'' vector would be extracted and form a ``small'' vector containing the
capacities of some arcs. The indices of the elements that are extracted from
the ``large'' vector are specified by the \code{SetFrom} set. This set could
be, for instance, the set $\{0, 3, 8, 11\}$. This means that the elements at
positions $0, 3, 8,$ and $11$ in the ``large'' vector are selected to form a
``small'' vector with four elements. This ``small'' vector would then be used
to change the capacities of some arcs of the \code{Network} object. The arcs
whose capacities would be modified could be specified by the \code{SetTo}
set. This could be the set $[2, 6)$, for instance, stating that the arcs with
indices $2, 3, 4,$ and $5$ would have their capacities changed according to
the ``small'' vector.

Usually, the \code{SetFrom} and the \code{SetTo} sets will have the same
cardinality, so that the $i$-th element of the \code{SetFrom} set will be
associated with the $i$-th element of the \code{SetTo} set. However, the
\code{SetFrom} set is also allowed to be smaller than the \code{SetTo} set. In
this case, the cardinality of the \code{SetTo} set must be a positive multiple
of the cardinality of the \code{SetFrom} set and the $i$-th element of the
\code{SetTo} set will be associated with the element of the \code{SetFrom} set
located at position $\lfloor i/r \rfloor$, where $r$ is the ratio between the
cardinalities of the \code{SetTo} and \code{SetFrom} sets.

Besides the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets, the \code{SimpleDataMapping}
also has a pointer to a function, which is invoked within the
\code{set\_data()} method. This is a function that receives, in particular, a
pointer to a \Block, the ``small'' vector, and the \code{SetTo} set. If the
\code{SetTo} set is a \code{Block::Subset}, then the type of this function is
\begin{verbatim}
    Block::FunctionType< typename std::vector< DataType >::const_iterator ,
                         SetTo && , bool >
\end{verbatim}
If the \code{SetTo} set is a \code{Block::Range}, then the type of this
function is
\begin{verbatim}
    Block::FunctionType< typename std::vector< DataType >::const_iterator ,
                         const SetTo & >
\end{verbatim}
A \code{Range} represents the closed-open interval $[a, b)$ set of indices
(integers), determined by the integers $a$ and $b$. A \code{Subset}
represents a set of indices by explicitly listing its elements. Please refer
to the definition of \code{Block::FunctionType} for completely understanding
the type of this function.

In the network example above, this function could be, for instance,
\begin{verbatim}
    void set_capacities( Network * network ,
                         std::vector<double>::const_iterator capacities ,
                         const Range & indices ,
                         c_ModParam , c_ModParam );
\end{verbatim}
Ignoring the details of the type of this function, this is a function that
receives a pointer to a \code{Network} object, a vector of capacities, and a
\code{Range} of indices. This function could be responsible for changing the
capacities of the arcs (of the given \code{Network} object) specified by the
\code{indices} parameter according to the given \code{capacities}.

As you can see, the function associated with a \code{SimpleDataMapping}
receives a pointer to a \Block as its first parameter. This is a pointer to
the caller object; the object that ``invokes'' the function. In the network
example, this would be a pointer to a Network object whose capacities would be
modified.

Finally, the \code{SimpleDataMapping} is also determined by the type of the
data of the ``small'' vector, the \code{DataType}.

Notice that a \code{SimpleDataMapping} is general enough in that it is not
only meant to change the data of some object, but perform arbitrary
computation defined by the function associated with this
\code{SimpleDataMapping}.

In summary, a \code{SimpleDataMapping} has the following template parameters:
\begin{itemize}
\item \code{SetFrom}: This is the type of the set that selects the appropriate
  data from data vector. It must be either \code{Block::Range} or
  \code{Block::Subset}.

\item \code{SetTo}: This is the type of the set that indicates which part of
  the data of the caller object that is affected. It must be either
  \code{Block::Range} or \code{Block::Set}.

\item \code{DataType}: This is the type of the data of the ``small'' vector
  (typically the type of the data that will be set in the caller object).

\item \code{Caller}: This is the type of the caller object, which is the
  object that will ``invoke'' the function. By default, \code{Caller} is
  \code{Block}.
\end{itemize}

Besides the description of a single \code{SimpleDataMapping}, we also provide
a description of a vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping}. In
\S \ref{sec:single_SimpleDataMapping} we present the description of a
single \code{SimpleDataMapping} and in
\S \ref{sec:vector_SimpleDataMapping} we present the description of a
vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping}.

\subsubsubsection{A single \code{SimpleDataMapping}}\label{sec:single_SimpleDataMapping}

\noindent
A \code{SimpleDataMapping} is described by the following \netCDF \variables
and \dimensions. These do not need to be in a specific \netCDF \group, unless
of course one has to specify more than one \code{SimpleDataMapping} in a
\group, in which case it is necessary to make specific \groups because the name
of a \variable/\dimension cannot be duplicated in a given \group.

\begin{itemize}

\item The one-dimensional \variable \code{SetSize}, an array of type
  \code{netCDF::NcUint} with two elements indicating the sizes (or types) of
  the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets. \code{SetSize[0]} indicates the
  size (or type) of the \code{SetFrom} set and \code{SetSize[1]} indicates the
  size (or type) of the \code{SetTo} set. For each $i \in \{0,1\}$, if
  \code{SetSize[i] == 0}, then the corresponding set is a
  \code{Range}. Otherwise, if $\code{SetSize[i]} \ne 0$, then the
  corresponding set is a \code{Subset} whose size is
  \code{SetSize[i]}. Notice, therefore, that \code{SetSize[i]} is not the size
  of the corresponding set when \code{SetSize[i] == 0}. In this case, it only
  indicates that the set is a \code{Range}, whose size (and elements) can be
  determined by the \cstr{SetElements} \variable. This \variable is
  optional. If it is not provided, then the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo}
  sets are assumed to be \code{Range}.

\item The one-dimensional \variable \cstr{SetElements}, of type
  \code{netCDF::NcUint}, containing the concatenation of the representations
  of the sets \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo}. A \code{Subset} is represented
  by a sequence of indices (which are the elements of the \code{Subset});
  while a \code{Range} is represented by two indices $a$ and $b$ such that the
  \code{Range} set is given by the integers in the closed-open interval $[a,
    b)$. For instance, if \code{SetFrom} is the \code{Subset} $\{3, 6, 8\}$
    and \code{SetTo} is the \code{Range} $[2, 5)$, then \cstr{SetElements}
      would be the array $(3, 6, 8, 2, 5)$.

\item The \variable \cstr{FunctionName}, whose type is
  \code{netCDF::NcString}, containing the name of the function as it is
  registered in the methods factory.

\item The \group \cstr{AbstractPath} containing the description of the
  \code{AbstractPath} representing the path to the caller object.

\end{itemize}

\subsubsubsection{Vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping}}\label{sec:vector_SimpleDataMapping}

\noindent
A vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping} is described by the following \netCDF
\variables and \dimensions. These do not need to be in a specific \netCDF
\group, unless of course one has to specify more than one vector of
\code{SimpleDataMapping} in a \group, in which case it is necessary to make
specific \groups because the name of a \variable/\dimension cannot be duplicated
in a given \group.

\begin{itemize}
\item The \cstr{NumberDataMappings} \dimension, indicating the number of
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}s that is present in the vector of
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}. This \dimension is optional. This dimension is
  optional. If it is not provided, then $\cstr{NumberDataMappings} = 0$ is
  assumed and every element in this \group is ignored.

\item The one-dimensional \variable \cstr{DataType}, of type
  \code{netCDF::NcChar} and indexed over the \cstr{NumberDataMappings}
  \dimension, specifying the type of the data that is associated with each
  \code{SimpleDataMapping} of the vector. This is the type of the data that
  can be set by the \code{SimpleDataMapping} (i.e., the \code{DataType}
  template parameter of \code{SimpleDataMapping}). This variable is
  optional. If it is not present, then the data type associated with each
  \code{SimpleDataMapping} in this vector is assumed to be \code{double}. If
  it is present then, for each $i \in \{0, \dots,
  \code{NumberDataMappings}-1\}$, \code{DataType[ i ]} is the type of the data
  associated with the $i$-th \code{SimpleDataMapping} and can be either `I' or
  `D', indicating that the type of the data is \code{int} or \code{double},
  respectively.

\item The one-dimensional \variable \code{SetSize}, an array of type
  \code{netCDF::NcUint} with size given by $2
  \times$\code{Number\-Data\-Mappings} indicating the size of the sets that
  define each \code{SimpleDataMapping} (the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo}
  sets). This variable is optional. If it is not present, then all sets are
  assumed to be \code{Range}. If it is present, then \code{SetSize[ 2i + k ]}
  is the size of the \code{SetFrom} set of the $i$-th \code{SimpleDataMapping}
  if $k = 0$ or the size of the \code{SetTo} set of the $i$-th
  \code{SimpleDataMapping} if $k = 1$. If \code{SetSize[ j ] == 0}, then the
  corresponding set is a \code{Range}. Otherwise, the corresponding set is a
  \code{Subset} of size \code{SetSize[ j ]}.

\item The one-dimensional \variable \cstr{SetElements}, of type
  \code{netCDF::NcUint}, containing the concatenation of the representations
  of the sets \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo}. A \code{Subset} is represented
  by a sequence of indices (which are the elements of the \code{Subset});
  while a \code{Range} is represented by two indices $a$ and $b$ such that the
  \code{Range} set is given by the integers in the closed-open interval $[a, b)$. If
    we let \code{SetFrom}$_i$ and \code{SetTo}$_i$ denote the representations
    of the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets of the $i$-th
    \code{SimpleDataMapping}, then \cstr{SetElements} is the array
    \[
      ( \code{SetFrom}_0 , \code{SetTo}_0 , \code{SetFrom}_1 , \code{SetTo}_1 , \dots, \code{SetFrom}_{N-1}, \code{SetTo}_{N-1} )
    \]
  where $N = \code{NumberDataMappings}$.

\item The one-dimensional variable \cstr{FunctionName}, of type
  \code{netCDF::NcString} and indexed over \code{NumberDataMappings},
  containing the names of the functions associated with each
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}. \code{FunctionName[ i ]} gives the name of the
  function (as registered in the methods factory) associated with the $i$-th
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}.

\item The one-dimensional variable \cstr{Caller}, of type
  \code{netCDF::NcChar} and indexed over \code{NumberDataMappings}, containing
  the types of the caller objects associated with each
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}. \code{Caller[ i ]} gives the type of the caller
  object associated with the $i$-th \code{SimpleDataMapping} and can be either
  `B', indicating that the caller is a \Block, or `F', indicating that the
  caller is a \Function. This \variable is optional. If it is not provided,
  then we assume that \code{Caller[ i ] = 'B'} for each $i \in \{0, \dots,
  \cstr{NumberDataMappings} - 1\}$, that is, we assume that all callers are
  \Blocks.

\item The \group \cstr{AbstractPath}, containing a vector of
  \code{AbstractPath} with the paths to the {\Block}s. The number of
  \code{AbstractPath} that this vector must contain is
  \cstr{NumberDataMappings}. The $i$-th \cstr{AbstractPath} in this vector of
  \code{AbstractPath} is the path to the \Block associated with the $i$-th
  \code{SimpleDataMapping}.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -

\section{File formats of existing \dBlock}

This section collects the description of the \netCDF file formats for some the implemented \dBlock in \SMSpp. As such, the section will be continuously updated over the course of the project as new \dBlock will be added, although non-backward-compatible changes to existing input formats will be kept to a minimum.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{MCFBlock}}

The \code{MCFBlock} class implements the \Block concept for the (linear) Min-Cost Flow (MCF) problem.

\smallskip
\noindent
The data of the problem consist of a (directed) graph $G = ( N , A )$ with $n = |N|$ nodes and $m = |A|$ (directed) arcs. Each node $i$ has a deficit $b[ i ]$, i.e., the amount of flow that is produced/consumed by the node: source nodes (which produce flow) have negative deficits and sink nodes (which consume flow) have positive deficits. Each arc $(i, j)$ has an upper capacity $U[ i , j ]$ and a linear cost coefficient $C[ i , j ]$. Flow variables $X[ i , j ]$ represents the amount of flow to be sent on arc $(i, j)$. Parallel arcs, i.e., multiple copies of the same arc $(i, j)$ are in general allowed; it is expected that they have different costs (for otherwise they can be merged into a unique arc), but this is not strictly enforced. Multiple copies of some arc $(i, j)$ can be seen as ``total'' flow cost on that arc being a piecewise-linear convex function. The formulation of the problem is:
%
\begin{align*}
 \min \; & \textstyle \sum_{ (i, j) \in A } C[ i , j ] X[ i, j ] \\[0.1cm]
 %
 & \textstyle
   \sum_{ (j, i) \in A } X[ j , i ] -
   \sum_{ (i, j) \in A } X[ i , j ] = b[ i ] & i \in N \\[0.1cm]
 %
 & 0 \leq X[ i , j ] \leq U[ i , j ] & (i, j) \in A
\end{align*}
%
The $n$ equations are the flow conservation constraints and the $2m$ inequalities are the flow nonnegativity and capacity constraints. At least one of the flow conservation constraints is redundant, as the demands must be balanced ($\sum_{ i \in N } b[ i ] = 0$); indeed, exactly $n$ - ConnectedComponents( $G$ ) flow conservation constraints are redundant, as demands must be balanced in each connected component of $G$.

\smallskip
\noindent
The graph $G$ is allowed to be ``partly dynamic''. The set of nodes and arcs that are input at the beginning are assumed not to be changed (save for changing costs, capacities, and deficits, and for arcs to be closed or opened). Then, new arcs and nodes, up to a set maximum, can be dynamically added or deleted. This means that the graph can be fully static (if the maximum number of dynamic arcs and nodes is set to zero) as well as fully dynamic (if the initial graph is empty).

\smallskip
\noindent
Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dBlock, the \group representing the \code{MCFBlock} should contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item the \dimension \cstr{NNodes} containing the number of nodes in the graph ($n$);
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{NArcs} containing the number of arcs in the graph ($m$);
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{C}, of type \code{double} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NArcs}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the upper capacity of the $i$-th arc in the graph, that whose starting node and ending node are specified by the \variable \cstr{SN} and \cstr{EN} (below);
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{U}, of type \code{double} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NArcs}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the cost of the $i$-th arc in the graph (the lower capacity being fixed to 0), that whose starting node and ending node are specified by the \variable \cstr{SN} and \cstr{EN} (below);
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{B}, of type \code{double} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NNodes}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the deficit of the i-th node in the graph (note that node names here go from $0$ to $n - 1$);
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{SN}, of type \code{int} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NArcs}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the starting node of the $i$-th arc in the graph (note that node names here go from $1$ to $n$, they are shifted by $1$ w.r.t.~to the indices used in the \cstr{B} variable);
 %
 \item the \variable \cstr{EN}, of type \code{int} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NArcs}; the $i$-th entry of the variable is assumed to contain the ending node of the $i$-th arc in the graph (note that node names here go from $1$ to $n$, they are shifted by $1$ w.r.t.~to the indices used in the \cstr{B} variable);
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{DynNNodes} containing the current number of dynamic nodes: all the nodes between $0$ and \cstr{NNodes} $-$ \cstr{DynNNodes} $- 1$ are static, i.e., they cannot be deleted (and re-created), whereas all those from \cstr{NNodes} + \cstr{DynNNode} to \cstr{NNodes} $- 1$ are dynamic, i.e., they can deleted and later on re-created;
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{DynNArcs} containing the current number of dynamic arcs: all the arcs between 0 and \cstr{NArcs} - \cstr{DynNArcs} $- 1$ are static, i.e., they cannot be deleted (and re-created), whereas all those from \cstr{NArcs} $+$ \cstr{DynNArcs} to \cstr{NArcs} $- 1$ are dynamic, i.e., they can deleted and later on re-created;
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{MaxDynNNodes} containing the maximum number of dynamic nodes in the graph (the maximum value that $n$ can take); data in the \code{MCFBlock} is allocated to accommodate for the fact that further \cstr{MaxDynNNodes} $-$ \cstr{DynNNodes} nodes can later on be dynamically created (and deleted); if \cstr{MaxDynNNodes} $<$ \cstr{DynNNodes} the \dimension is ignored and \cstr{NNodes} is taken as the maximum overall number of nodes;
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{MaxDynNArcs} containing the maximum number of dynamic arcs in the graph (the maximum value that $m$ can take): data in the \code{MCFBlock} is allocated to accommodate for the fact that further \cstr{MaxDynNArcs} $-$ \cstr{DynNArcs} arcs can later on be dynamically created (and deleted); if \cstr{MaxDynNArcs} $<$ \cstr{DynNArcs} the \dimension is ignored and \cstr{NArcs} is taken as the maximum overall number of arcs.
\end{itemize}

\smallskip
\noindent
The two \dimensions \cstr{NNodes} and \cstr{NArcs} are mandatory, such as are the two \variables \cstr{SN} and \cstr{EN}. The three other \variables are optional. If \cstr{C} is missing, all arc costs are assumed to be $0$. If \cstr{U} is missing, all arc capacities are assumed to be infinite. If \cstr{B} is missing, all node deficits are assumed to be $0$. Finally, all the \dimensions \cstr{DynNNodes}, \cstr{DynNArcs}, \cstr{MaxDynNNodes} and \cstr{MaxDynNArcs} are optional: if they are missing they are treated as being $0$ (this happening for all four means that the graph is ``fully static'' and cannot be changed).

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{UCBlock}}\label{sec:UCBlock}

The class \code{UCBlock}, implements the \Block concept [see Block.h] for the Unit Commitment (UC) problem in electrical power production. This is typically a short-term (across for instance one week or one day time horizon) \highlight{deterministic} problem regarding finding an optimal schedule of the production of electrical generators satisfying a (large) set of technical constraints.

\smallskip
The model is quite flexible due to the fact that different types of units and network constraints can be used by means of the fact that the class manages son \Block of type \code{UnitBlock} and \code{NetworkBlock}. Also \code{UCBlock} handles a reasonably large variety of constraints, regarding not only active power but also primary and secondary reserve and inertia. Admittedly, some choices in \code{UCBlock} are quite specific, but all the \cstr{nonstandard} aspects of UC can be switched away from the model (by simply not providing the data describing them). The main elements that \code{UCBlock} handles are:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item The time horizon of the problem, i.e., a discrete set of (typically, equally-spaced) time instants at which decisions are made (like, the $24$ hours in a day);
 %
 \item A set of electricity generating units, represented by derived classes of the base class \code{UnitBlock};
 %
 \item A set of \code{NetworkBlock}, one for each time instant in the time horizon, which represent the constraints on the electricity demand satisfaction and the technical constraints on the transmission network. These can be basically \cstr{empty} if the capacity of the transmission network is such as to never really impact generation decisions (a \cstr{bus});
 %
 %\item An optional set of \code{HeatBlock}, each representing the satisfaction of some specific \cstr{type of heat} on a close geographical area by heat-generating units possibly coupled with a heat storage. The link with the rest of the UC model lies in the fact that some of the heat-generating units in a \code{HeatBlock} may also be electricity generating ones (i.e., a \code{UnitBlock}); actually, the same \code{UnitBlock} can generate heat of \cstr{different types}, and therefore appear as a heat-generating units in more than one \code{HeatBlock}.
\end{itemize}

\smallskip
\noindent
Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dBlock, the \group should contain the following:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item the \dimension \cstr{TimeHorizon} containing the number of time steps in the problem;
 %
 \item the \dimension \cstr{NumberUnits} containing the number of units (\code{UnitBlock}) in the problem;
 %
 \item the \groups \cstr{UnitBlock\_0}, \cstr{UnitBlock\_1}, \dots, \cstr{UnitBlock\_n} with \code{n == NumberUnits - 1}, containing each one \code{UnitBlock} corresponding to one or more electricity generating unit (electrical generator). It is an error if the corresponding groups are not there. Each \code{UnitBlock} can have more than one electrical generator (cf. \code{UnitBlock::get\_number\_generators()}), a value that is useful in the following (cf. \cstr{GeneratorNode}) is the total number of those, which we will refer to as \cstr{NumberElectricalGenerators}. This is computed by just calling \code{get\_number\_generators()} on each of the \code{UnitBlock} and summing all the results. Clearly, \code{NumberElectricalGenerators >= NumberUnits} and indeed, most of the \code{UnitBlock} can be expected to have just one electrical generator. If this happens for all the units then the \code{NumberElectricalGenerators ==  NumberUnits}. If, instead, some \code{UnitBlock} (like cascades of hydro generators or combined cycle plants) actually has more than one electrical generator, then \code{NumberElectricalGenerators > NumberUnits} (each \code{UnitBlock} must have at least one). This is because some \code{UnitBlock} (like cascades of hydro generators or combined cycle plants) can actually have more than one electrical generator in it (but each \code{UnitBlock} must have at least one). It is then useful (cf. \cstr{GeneratorNode}) to be able to assign a unique index \code{g = 0, 1, \dots, NumberElectricalGenerators - 1} to each of the electrical generators in the \code{UCBlock}. When \code{NumberElectricalGenerators == NumberUnits}, the index is the same as \code{i = 0, 1, \dots, NumberUnits - 1}(there is a one-to-one correspondence between \code{UnitBlock} and electrical generators). When, instead, \code{NumberElectricalGenerators > NumberUnits}, a mapping must be defined. The mapping is the obvious one: \code{UnitBlock} have an ordering \code{i = 0, 1, \dots, NumberUnits - 1} (cf. the groups \cstr{UnitBlock\_0}, \cstr{UnitBlock\_1}, \dots, above), and the electrical generators into each \code{UnitBlock} also have some natural ordering (corresponding to the columns of the matrices of variables, cf. e.g. \code{UnitBlock::get\_commitment()}). Thus, in general the mapping is:
 \begin{itemize}
 \item electrical generator \code{0 =} first generator of \code{UnitBlock\_0}
 \item electrical generator \code{1 =} second generator of \code{UnitBlock\_0}
 \item \code{\dots}
 \item electrical generator \code{k-1 =} $k$-th generator of \code{UnitBlock\_0} \\ (\code{k = UnitBlock\_0->get\_number\_generators()})
 \item electrical generator \code{k =} first generator of \code{UnitBlock\_1}
 \item electrical generator \code{k + 1 =} second generator of \code{UnitBlock\_1}
 \item \code{\dots}
 \end{itemize}
 \smallskip
 \noindent
 which of course boils down to \cstr{g = i} when each UnitBlock has exactly one electrical generator;
 %
 %\item the \dimension \cstr{NumberHeatBlocks} containing the number of heat blocks in the problem. The \dimension is optional: if it is not provided then it is taken to be $0$, which means that there is no heat block in the problem;
 %
 %\item the \groups \cstr{HeatBlock\_0}, \cstr{HeatBlock\_1}, \dots, \cstr{HeatBlock\_n} with \code{n == NumberHeatBlocks - 1}, containing each one a \code{HeatBlock}. When \code{NumberHeatBlocks == 0}, these groups need not be there since they are not read. If, instead, \code{NumberHeatBlocks > 0}, it is an error if the corresponding groups are not there. Since each HeatBlock can have more than one heat generator (cf. \code{HeatBlock::get\_number\_heat\_generators()}), a value that is useful in the following (cf. \cstr{HeatSet}) is the total number of those. We will refer to such number as \cstr{NumberHeatGenerators}, which is computed by just calling \code{get\_number\_heat\_generators()} on each of the \code{HeatBlock} and summing all the results. Clearly, \code{NumberHeatGenerators >= NumberHeatBlock}. Some of the \code{HeatBlock} may have just one heat generator; if this happens for all the heat blocks (but this is not likely), then \code{NumberHeatGenerators ==  NumberHeatBlocks}. It is then useful (cf. \cstr{HeatNode}) to be able to assign a unique index \code{h = 0, 1, \dots, NumberHeatGenerators - 1} to each of the heat generators in the \code{UCBlock}. When \code{NumberHeatGenerators == NumberHeatBlocks} the index is the same as \code{b = 0, 1, \dots, NumberHeatBlock - 1} (there is a one-to-one correspondence between \code{HeatBlock} and heat generators, but this is not likely to happen). When, instead, \code{NumberHeatGenerators > HeatBlock}, a mapping must be defined. The mapping is the obvious one: \code{HeatBlock} have an ordering \code{b = 0, 1, \dots, NumberHeatBlocks - 1} (cf. the groups \cstr{HeatBlock\_0}, \cstr{HeatBlock\_1}, \dots, above), and the heat generators into each \code{HeatBlock} also have some natural ordering (corresponding to the columns of the matrices of variables, cf. e.g. \code{HeatBlock::get\_heat()}. Thus, in general the mapping is:
% \begin{itemize}
%  \item heat generator \code{0 =} first generator of \code{HeatBlock\_0}
%  \item heat generator \code{1 =} second generator of \code{HeatBlock\_0}
%  \item \code{\dots}
%  \item heat generator \code{k =} $k$-th generator of \code{HeatBlock\_0} \\ (\code{k = HeatBlock\_0->get\_number\_heat\_units()})
%  \item heat generator \code{k + 1 =} first generator of \code{HeatBlock\_1}
%  \item heat generator \code{k + 2 =} second generator of \code{HeatBlock\_1}
%  \item \code{\dots}
% \end{itemize}
% \smallskip
% \noindent
