The time horizon is subdivided into \code{NumberIntervals = k} of the form $[ 0 , i_1 ]$,$[ i_1 + 1 , i_2 ]$, \dots $[ i_{k-1} + 1, \code{TimeHorizon} - 1 ]$; \cstr{ChangeIntervals} then has to contain $[ i_1 , i_2 , \dots i_{k-1} ]$ . Note that, therefore, \cstr{ChangeIntervals} has one significant value less than \cstr{NumberIntervals}, which means that \code{ChangeIntervals[ NumberIntervals - 1 ]} is ignored. Anyway, the whole variable is ignored if either \cstr{NumberIntervals} \code{<= 1} (such as if it is not defined), or \cstr{NumberIntervals} \code{>=} \cstr{TimeHorizon};
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{TotalHeatDemand}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{TimeHorizon}: entry \code{TotalHeatDemand[ t ]} is assumed to contain the total heat demand of this heat block to be satisfied for the corresponding time instant $t$. Note that this variable does \highlight{not} use the \cstr{NumberIntervals} / \cstr{ChangeIntervals} system, as demand is usually changing from one time instant to the next;
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{CostHeatUnit}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over two \dimensions. The first \dimension can have size $1$ or \cstr{NumberIntervals} (if \cstr{NumberIntervals} is not provided, then the size can also be \cstr{TimeHorizon}). The second \dimension has size \cstr{NumberHeatUnits}. This is meant to represent the matrix \code{CHU[ t , i ]} which is assumed to contain the unitary cost of heat production of heat-producing unit $i$ for time instant $t$. If the first \dimension has size $1$, then the cost is the same for all time instants (for the same unit). Otherwise, \code{CostHeatUnit[ h , i ]} is the fixed value of \code{CHU[ t , i ]} for all $t$ in the interval \code{[ ChangeIntervals[ h - 1 ], ChangeIntervals[ h ] ]}, with the assumption that \code{ChangeIntervals[ - 1 ] = 0};
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{MinHeatProduction}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over two \dimensions. The first \dimension can have size $1$ or \cstr{NumberIntervals} (if \cstr{NumberIntervals} is not provided, then the size can also be \cstr{TimeHorizon}). The second \dimension has size \cstr{NumberHeatUnits}. This is meant to represent the matrix \cstr{MinHP[ t , i ]} which is assumed to contain the minimum heat production of heat-producing unit $i$ for time instant $t$. The variable is optional; if it is not provided at all, it is intended \cstr{MinHP[ t , i ] == 0} for all $i$ and $t$. If the first \dimension has size $1$, then the minimum heat production is the same for all time instants (for the same unit). Otherwise, \code{MinHeatProduction[ h , i ]} is the fixed value of \code{MinHP[ t , i ]} for all $t$ in the interval \code{[ ChangeIntervals[ h - 1 ], ChangeIntervals[ h ] ]}, with the assumption that \code{ChangeIntervals[ - 1 ] = 0};
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{MaxHeatProduction}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over two \dimensions. The first \dimension can have size $1$ or \cstr{NumberIntervals} (if \cstr{NumberIntervals} is not provided, then the size can also be \cstr{TimeHorizon}). The second \dimension has size \cstr{NumberHeatUnits}. This is meant to represent the matrix \cstr{MaxHP[ t , i ]} which is assumed to contain the maximum heat production of heat-producing unit $i$ for time instant $t$. It is assumed \code{MaxHP[ t , i ] >= MinHP[ t , i ] >= 0} for all $i$ and $t$, with strict inequality holding for at least some $t$ for each unit $i$ (otherwise the production of unit $i$ is fixed and there is nothing to decide). The variable is optional; if it is not provided at all, it is intended \cstr{MaxHP[ t , i ] == 0} for all $i$ and $t$. If the first \dimension has size $1$, then the maximum heat production is the same for all time instants (for the same unit). Otherwise, \code{MaxHeatProduction[ h , i ]} is the fixed value of \code{MaxHP[ t , i ]} for all $t$ in the interval \code{[ ChangeIntervals[ h - 1 ], ChangeIntervals[ h ] ]}, with the assumption that \code{ChangeIntervals[ - 1 ] = 0};
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{MinHeatStorage}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and either of size 1 or indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NumberIntervals} (if \cstr{NumberIntervals} is not provided, then the size can also be \cstr{TimeHorizon}). This is meant to represent the vector \code{MinHS[ t ]} which, for each time instant $t$, contains the minimum heat storage of this \code{HeatBlock}. The variable is optional, if it is not provided at all it is intended that \code{MinHS[ t ] == 0} for all $t$. If the variable has size $1$, then the minimum heat storage is the same for all time instants. Otherwise, \code{MinHeatStorage[ h ]} is the fixed value of \code{MinHS[ t ]} for all $t$ in the interval \code{[ ChangeIntervals[ h - 1 ], ChangeIntervals[ h ] ]}, with the assumption that \code{ChangeIntervals[ - 1 ] = 0};
% %
% \item the \variable \cstr{MaxHeatStorage}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and either of size 1 or indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NumberIntervals} (if \cstr{NumberIntervals} is not provided, then the size can also be \cstr{TimeHorizon}). This is meant to represent the vector \code{MaxHS[ t ]} which, for each time instant $t$, contains the maximum heat storage of this \code{HeatBlock}. The variable is optional, if it is not provided at all it is intended that \code{MaxHS[ t ] == 0} for all $t$  and since it's assumed that \code{MaxHS[ t ] >= MinHS[ t ] >= 0} for all $t$, this means that there is no heat storage in this \code{HeatBlock}. If the variable has size $1$, then the maximum heat storage is the same for all time instants. Otherwise, \code{MaxHeatStorage[ h ]} is the fixed value of \code{MaxHS[ t ]} for all $t$ in the interval \code{[ ChangeIntervals[ h - 1 ], ChangeIntervals[ h ] ]}, with the assumption that \code{ChangeIntervals[ - 1 ] = 0};
% %
% \item the scalar \variable \cstr{InitialHeatAvailable}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and not indexed over any \dimension, which indicates the amount of heat in the storage at the beginning of the first time instant in this \code{HeatBlock}. It is assumed that \cstr{MaxHS[ 0 ] >= InitialHeatAvailable >= MinHS[ 0 ]}. This variable is optional, if it is not provided it is taken to be \code{InitialHeatAvailable == MinHS[ 0 ]}. If there is no heat storage (say, \code{MaxHeatStorage} is not defined) then this variable is not read, because it is not used;
% %
% \item the scalar \variable \cstr{StoringHeatRho}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and not indexed over any \dimension, which indicates the inefficiency of storing heat in the heat storage (if any) in this \code{HeatBlock}. This variable is optional and it must always be \code{StoringHeatRho <= 1}, if it is not provided it is taken to be \code{StoringHeatRho == 1}. If there is no heat storage (say, MaxHeatStorage is not defined) then this variable is not read, because it is not used;
% %
% \item the scalar \variable \cstr{ExtractingHeatRho}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and not indexed over any \dimension, and which indicates the inefficiency of extracting heat from the heat storage (if any) in this \code{HeatBlock}. This variable is optional and it must always be \code{ExtractingHeatRho >= 1}, if it is not provided it is taken to be \code{ExtractingHeatRho == 1}. If there is no heat storage (say, \code{MaxHeatStorage} is not defined) then this variable is not read, because it is not used;
% %
% \item the scalar \variable \cstr{KeepingHeatRho}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and not indexed over any \dimension, which indicates the double of keeping heat in the heat storage (if any) in this \code{HeatBlock}. This variable is optional and it must always be \code{KeepingHeatRho <= 1}, if it is not provided it is taken to be \code{KeepingHeatRho == 1}. If there is no heat storage (say, \code{MaxHeatStorage} is not defined) then this variable is not read, because it is not used;
%\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{SDDPBlock}}\label{sec:SDDPBlock}

The \code{SDDPBlock} is a class that derives from \Block and represents a
multistage stochastic programming problem of the form
\[
   \min_{x_0 \in \mathcal{X}^{n_0}} f_0(x_0) +
   \mathbb{E} \left \lbrack
   \min_{x_1 \in \mathcal{X}^{n_1}} f_1(x_1) +
   \mathbb{E} \left \lbrack \dots +
   \mathbb{E} \left \lbrack
   \min_{x_{T-1} \in \mathcal{X}^{n_{T-1}}} f_{T-1}(x_{T-1})
   \right\rbrack \right\rbrack\right\rbrack,
\]
where $ T $ is called the time horizon, $\mathcal{X}^{n_t} \equiv
\mathcal{X}^{n_t}(x_{t-1}, \xi_t) \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{n_t}$ for each $t \in
\{0, \dots, T-1\}$, and $ \xi = \{ \xi_t \}_{t \in \{1, \dots, T-1\}} $ is a
stochastic process. Notice that $ x_{-1} $ and $ \xi_0 $ are
deterministic. For each $ t \in \{0, \dots, T-1\}$, we call
\[
   \min_{x_t \in \mathcal{X}^{n_t}} f_t(x_t) +
   \mathcal{V}_{t+1}(x_t, \xi_t)
\]
the problem associated with stage $ t $, where
\[
   \mathcal{V}_{t+1}(x_t, \xi_t) =
     \mathbb{E}
       \left\lbrack
         V_{t+1}(x_t, \xi_{t+1}) \mid \xi_t
       \right\rbrack
\]
is the (expected value) cost-to-go function (also called value
function, future value function, future cost function), with $
\mathcal{V}_{T} \equiv 0 $ and
\[
   V_{t}(x_{t-1}, \xi_{t}) =
   \min_{x_t \in \mathcal{X}^{n_t}} f_t(x_t) +
   \mathcal{V}_{t+1}(x_t, \xi_t)
\]
with given $ x_{-1} $ and (deterministic) $ \xi_0$. We consider an
approximation to the problem associated with stage $ t \in \{0, \dots, T-1\} $
as the problem
\begin{equation}
   \min_{x_t \in \mathcal{X}^{n_t}} f_t(x_t) +
   \mathcal{P}_{t+1}(x_t)
   \label{eq:approx_prob_sddp}
\end{equation}
where $ \mathcal{P}_{t+1}(x_t) $ is a polyhedral
function, i.e., it is a function of the form
\[
   \mathcal{P}_{t+1}(x_t) = \max_{i \in \{1,\dots,k_t\}}
                                    \{ d_{t,i}^{\top}x_t + e_{t,i} \}
\]
with $ d_{t,i} \in \mathbb{R}^{n_t} $ and $ e_{t,i} \in
\mathbb{R} $ for each $ i \in \{1,\dots,k_t\} $.

An \code{SDDPBlock} is then characterized by the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item It has a time horizon $ T $.

\item It has $ T $ sub-{\Block}s, each one being a \code{StochasticBlock}. The
  $t$-th sub-\Block represents an approximation to the problem associated with
  stage $ t $ as defined in~\eqref{eq:approx_prob_sddp}.

\item It has pointers to $ T - 1 $ \code{PolyhedralFunction}. The $t$-th
  \code{PolyhedralFunction} represents the function $ \mathcal{P}_{t+1} $
  in~\eqref{eq:approx_prob_sddp} and, therefore, must be defined in the $t$-th
  sub-\Block of this \code{SDDPBlock} or in any of the sub-{\Block}s of that
  sub-\Block, recursively.

\item It has a set of scenarios $\mathcal{S}$. Each scenario in $\mathcal{S}$
  is represented by a vector of \code{double} and spans all the time horizon
  $T$. Each vector is divided into $T$ parts, each one being associated with a
  stage of the multistage problem. Let $S$ denote a vector representing a
  scenario in $\mathcal{S}$. Then, $S$ is defined as
  \[
    S = ( S_0 , \dots, S_{T-1} )
  \]
  where $S_t$ is a sub-vector of $S$ with size $s_t$, for each $t \in \{ 0,
  \dots, T-1 \}$, and is associated with the sub-problem at stage $t$, i.e.,
  it provides data for the $t$-th sub-\Block of this \code{SDDPBlock}. We say
  that $S_t$ represents the $t$-th sub-scenario of the scenario represented
  by $S$.

  We assume that the sub-scenarios are organized in such a way that related
  random data appear in contiguous areas of the sub-scenario. For instance,
  suppose that the random data is associated with demand, inflow, and wind
  power. In this case, the data related to demand should be a contiguous
  sub-vector $D_t$ of the sub-scenario associated with stage $t$, as well as
  that related to inflow ($F_t$) and wind power ($W_t$). In this example, the
  sub-scenario $S_t$ could be organized as
  \[
  S_t = ( D_t , F_t , W_t ).
  \]
  We say that this sub-scenario has three groups of related random data. The
  order in which the groups of related random data appear in $S_t$ is not
  relevant. We could have, for instance,
  \[
  S_t = ( W_t , D_t , F_t ).
  \]
  But the sub-scenario associated with stage $t$ must respect the same order
  for each scenario in $\mathcal{S}$.
%  Ideally, the number of groups should not be greater than three. If the
%  number of groups is greater than three, then they will be arbitrarily
%  combined in order to obtain at most three groups.
\end{enumerate}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \Block, an \SMSpp \netCDF
\group for an \code{SDDPBlock} contains the following:
\begin{itemize}
\item The \cstr{TimeHorizon} \dimension, containing the time horizon.

\item The description of the sub-{\Block}s of the \code{SDDPBlock}. This is
  given by the sub-groups \cstr{StochasticBlock} and
  \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t}, for each $t \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{TimeHorizon} -
  1 \}$. These sub-groups are optional, but they cannot be all absent. If
  \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} is not provided for some $t \in \{0, \dots,
  \cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1 \}$, then the \cstr{StochasticBlock} \group must be
  provided and contain a complete description of the $t$-th sub-\Block of this
  \code{SDDPBlock}. If the \cstr{StochasticBlock} \group is not provided, then
  \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} must be provided for each $t \in \{0, \dots,
  \cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1 \}$ and contain a complete description of the $t$-th
  sub-\Block of this \code{SDDPBlock}.

  If \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} is provided but the description of its inner
  \Block is not provided, then the \cstr{StochasticBlock} \group must be
  provided and contain the description of an inner \Block of a
  \code{StochasticBlock}. In this case, the description of the inner \Block
  provided in the \cstr{StochasticBlock} \group will be used to construct the
  inner \Block of the \code{StochasticBlock} described by the
  \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} \group.

  If \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} is provided but the description of its vector
  of \code{DataMapping} is not provided, then if the \cstr{StochasticBlock}
  \group is provided and contains a description of a vector of
  \code{DataMapping}, then it is used to construct the vector of
  \code{DataMapping} of the \code{StochasticBlock} described by the
  \cstr{StochasticBlock\_t} \group.

\item The \cstr{AbstractPath} \group containing the description of a vector of
  \code{AbstractPath} as described in \S \ref{sec:AbstractPath}. The number of
  \code{AbstractPath} must be equal to either 1 or $\cstr{TimeHorizon} -
  1$. If the number of \code{AbstractPath} is $\cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1$ then
  the $i$-th \code{AbstractPath} in this vector must be the path to the
  \code{PolyhedralFunction} associated with the $i$-th sub-\Block of this
  \code{SDDPBlock}. The $i$-th \code{AbstractPath} is taken with respect to
  the inner \Block of the $i$-th sub-\Block of this \code{SDDPBlock}. If the
  number of \code{AbstractPath} in this vector is 1, then all paths are
  assumed to be equal: for each $i \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{TimeHorizon}-1\}$,
  the provided \code{AbstractPath} will be the path to the
  \code{PolyhedralFunction} associated with the $i$-th sub-\Block of this
  \code{SDDPBlock} (taken with respect to the inner \Block of this $i$-th
  sub-\Block).

\item The \cstr{NumberScenarios} \dimension specifying the number of
  scenarios.
 
\item The \cstr{ScenarioSize} \dimension containing the size of a single
  scenario, which spans all stages.
 
\item The \cstr{SubScenarioSize} \variable, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over \dimension \cstr{TimeHorizon}. This dimension is
  optional. If it is not provided, then all sub-scenarios are assumed to have
  the same size, i.e., $ s_t = (\cstr{ScenarioSize} / \cstr{TimeHorizon})$ for
  all $ t \in \{ 0, \dots, \cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1 \}$, and \cstr{ScenarioSize}
  is a multiple of \cstr{TimeHorizon}. If this \dimension is provided, then
  \code{SubScenarioSize[t]} is the size of the sub-scenario associated with
  stage $t$, i.e., $s_t = \code{SubScenarioSize[t]}$, for each $t \in \{ 0,
  \dots, \cstr{TimeHorizon}-1 \}$. In the latter case, the following must
  hold:
  \[
    \cstr{ScenarioSize} = \sum_{t = 0}^{\cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1}
    \cstr{SubScenarioSize}[t].
  \]

\item The two-dimensional \variable \cstr{Scenarios} of type
  \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over the dimensions
  \cstr{NumberScenarios} and \cstr{ScenarioSize}, containing the
  scenarios. The $i$-th row of \cstr{Scenarios} contains the $i$-th scenario,
  so that \code{Scenarios[ i ][ j ]} is the $j$-th component of the $i$-th
  scenario.

\item The \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups} \dimension containing the number of
  groups of related random data within each sub-scenario. This \dimension is
  optional. If it is not provided, then we assume that there is a single group
  of related random data. Also, this \dimension is meaningful only if all
  sub-scenarios have the same size.

\item The \cstr{SizeRandomDataGroups} \variable, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups} dimension, containing the
  size of each group of related random data in each sub-scenario. For each $i
  \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups} - 1\}$,
  \code{NumberRandomDataGroups[i]} is the size of the $i$-th group of a
  sub-scenario. This \variable is optional. It is required only if
  \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups} is provided and $\cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups}
  > 1$.

\item The \cstr{StateSize} \variable, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint} and being
  either a scalar or a one-dimensional array indexed over \cstr{TimeHorizon}
  dimension, specifying the sizes of the states at each stage. If this
  \variable is a scalar, then all states are assumed to have the same size
  given by \cstr{StateSize}. If it is an array then, for each $t \in
  \{0,\dots,\cstr{TimeHorizon}-1\}$, \cstr{StateSize}\code{[t]} contains the
  size of the state at stage $t$. The state being a vector, its size is the
  dimension of the space in which it lies.

%\item The \cstr{TotalStateSize} \dimension, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint},
%  specifying the sum of the sizes of the states of each stage. This dimension
%  is optional.

\item The \cstr{AdmissibleState} \variable, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble},
  containing an admissible state for each stage. An admissible state for a
  stage is an state which makes the problem at that stage feasible.
  If \cstr{StateSize} is scalar and \cstr{AdmissibleState} has
    \dimension \cstr{StateSize}, then all stages are assumed to have the same
    admissible state given by \cstr{AdmissibleState}. Otherwise,
    \cstr{AdmissibleState} contains the concatenation of the states for all
    stages as follows.
    \begin{itemize}
    \item If \cstr{StateSize} is scalar then, for each $t \in \{ 0, \dots,
      \cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1\}$, an admissible state for stage $t$ is given by
      \[
      ( \code{AdmissibleState[}s_t\code{]}, \dots, \cstr{AdmissibleState[}s_t
        + \code{StateSize - 1]} ),
      \]
      where $s_t = t \times \cstr{StateSize}$. In this case,
      \cstr{AdmissibleState} must have size $\cstr{TimeHorizon} \times
      \cstr{StateSize}$.

    \item If \cstr{StateSize} is a one-dimensional array then, for each $t \in
      \{ 0, \dots, \cstr{TimeHorizon} - 1\}$, an admissible state for stage
      $t$ is given by
      \[
      ( \code{AdmissibleState[}s_t\code{]}, \dots, \cstr{AdmissibleState[}s_t
        + \code{StateSize[t] - 1]}),
      \]
      where $s_t = \sum_{i=0}^{t-1} \cstr{StateSize[i]}$. In this case,
      \cstr{AdmissibleState} must have size equal to
      \[
      \sum_{i=0}^{\cstr{TimeHorizon} -1} \cstr{StateSize[i]}.
      \]
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{StochasticBlock}}\label{sec:StochasticBlock}

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \Block, an \SMSpp \netCDF
\group for a \code{StochasticBlock} contains the following:
\begin{itemize}
\item The \group \cstr{Block}, containing the description of the inner
  \Block. This \group is optional. If it is not provided, then the inner \Block
  must be provided by other means.

\item The description of a vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping}, containing the
  \code{DataMapping}s associated with this \code{StochasticBlock}. This is
  optional. For the time being, each provided \code{DataMapping} is expected
  to be a \code{SimpleDataMapping}. Moreover, the inner \Block of this
  \code{StochasticBlock} will serve as the reference \Block for both the
  serialization and deserialization of each \code{SimpleDataMapping}.
\end{itemize}

In order to better understand what the different elements, a comment about
\code{StochasticBlock} is in order. The \code{StochasticBlock} class is meant
to represent a \Block whose data may be stochastic. The idea is that any
\Block could have its stochastic version without any changes in its
implementation. The \code{StochasticBlock} comes to facilitate this process. A
\code{StochasticBlock} is characterized by the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item It has exactly one sub-\Block (called here as the inner \Block), which
  is the \Block that is becoming stochastic. This can be any \dBlock.

\item It is aware that some data of the inner \Block is stochastic (and may be
  subject to changes) and that the value for this data is represented by a
  vector of \code{double}. An instance of this vector is called a scenario for
  the stochastic data.

\item It has a set of \code{DataMapping}. This is used to both identify the
  stochastic data in the inner \Block and modify the values of this data. The
  inner \Block may have different types of stochastic data, located in
  different sub-{\Block}s. A \code{DataMapping} is meant to represent one
  piece of the stochastic data.

  To understand how it could be used, we present the following simple
  example. Consider a \Block $B$ which would be the inner \Block of a
  \code{StochasticBlock}. This \Block $B$ may have all sorts of data. We could
  have a stochastic version of $B$ by selecting some of its data to become
  uncertainties. Let us suppose that $B$ has a vector that represents some
  demand over time (from time $0$ to $T-1$). One could establish that the
  demand at times 0, 3, and 8 is stochastic. Suppose also that $B$ has a
  sub-\Block $B_1$ that has a vector representing costs to produce certain
  goods. One could also establish that the cost to produce good number 6 is
  stochastic. In this example, the stochastic data is formed by the demand of
  \Block $B$ at times 0, 3, and 8, and the cost to produce number 6 of \Block
  $B_1$. \code{DataMapping} can then be used to identify each of those
  uncertainties.

  We could say that a vector of length 4 is a scenario for this stochastic
  data such that the first three elements of this vector are values for the
  demands and the fourth element is a value for the cost of producing good
  number 6. We could use a \code{DataMapping} to identify that demand as
  stochastic data. To this end, we need two sets. One set identifies what part
  of the scenario (indices of that vector) is associated with the demand and
  the second set identifies what part of the demand of $B$ is stochastic. In
  this example, the first set would be $S_1 = \{0, 1, 2\}$, which states that
  the values for the demand are present at positions 0, 1, and 2 of the
  scenario vector, and the second set would be $S_2 = \{0, 3, 8\}$, which
  states that the demands that are stochastic are those at positions 0, 3, and
  8. We also need a way of modifying this data in $B$. For this, it suffices a
  method in $B$ that can be used to update its demand (see
  \code{SimpleDataMapping} in \S \ref{sec:SimpleDataMapping}), let us say one
  called \code{set\_demand(S2, vector)}.

  Another \code{DataMapping} could be used to identify the cost of producing
  good number 6 as stochastic. This \code{DataMapping} would have $S_1 =
  \{4\}$ as the first set (stating that the value for this data is at position
  4 of the scenario vector) and $S_2 = \{5\}$ as the second one (indicating
  which good cost is stochastic). Again, we need a way to update this cost in
  $B_1$, which could be done, for instance, if $B_1$ provides a method like
  \code{set\_cost(S2, vector)}.

\item It provides a method called \code{set\_data()} which has a scenario (a
  vector) as parameter and that sets the data of the inner \Block according to
  the set of \code{DataMapping} present in the \code{StochasticBlock}. In the
  example above, when the \code{set\_data()} method of \code{StochasticBlock}
  is called, the values for the demands and the good cost are updated with the
  given values in the scenario.

  In fact, the data of the inner \Block does not need to be stochastic in any
  sense. What this class provides is a means to set the value of the data of
  its inner \Block.
\end{enumerate}
A \code{StochasticBlock} should have a probability distribution (or some kind
of partial stochastic process) that describes the uncertainty in it. However,
for the moment, it is not supported by this class and this feature will be
implemented later on. Typically, an object of this class would be used in
conjunction with a scenario generator and the \code{set\_data()} method of
this object would be called to consider a particular scenario.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{BendersBlock}}

A \code{BendersBlock} is a \Block whose \Objective is an \code{FRealObjective}
whose \Function is a \code{BendersBFunction}. Moreover, it has a vector of
\code{ColVariable} which are the active \Variable of that \code{BendersBFunction}.

Besides the mandatory \cstr{type} attribute of any \dBlock, an \SMSpp
\netCDF \group for a \code{BendersBlock} contains the following:
\begin{itemize}
\item The \dimension \cstr{NumVar} containing the number of \code{ColVariable}
  of the \code{BendersBlock}.

\item The \group \cstr{BendersBFunction} containing the description of the
  \code{BendersBFunction} that is the \Function of the \code{FRealObjective}
  of the \code{BendersBlock}.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {\code{BendersBFunction}}\label{sec:BendersBFunction}

The \code{BendersBFunction} is a convenience class implementing the
``abstract'' concept of a Benders function of ``any''
\Block. \code{BendersBFunction} derives from \textbf{both} \code{C05Function}
and \Block.

The main ingredients of a \code{BendersBFunction} are the following:
\begin{enumerate}
\item A ``base'' \Block $B$, representing an optimization problem of the form
  \begin{equation}
    \min \{ c(y) : w \le E(y) \le z, y \in Y \}, \tag{B}
    \label{eq:B}
  \end{equation}
   where $c$, $w$, and $z$ are vectors of appropriate sizes, $E$ is a function
   of $y$, and $Y$ is a convex set. Let $E_i$ denote the $i$-th component of
   $E$. If $E_i$ is nonlinear, then it must be either convex (in which case we
   must have $w = -\infty$) or concave (in which case we must have $z =
   +\infty$). This will be the one, and only, sub-\Block of
   \code{BendersBFunction} (when ``seen'' as a \Block).

\item A matrix $A$ with $m$ rows and $n$ columns, a vector $b$ with $m$ rows,
  and a vector of pairs $[ ( C_i , S_i ) ]_{i \in I}$, each pair being formed
  by a pointer to a \code{RowConstraint} of \Block $B$ and a
  \code{ConstraintSide}, where $I = \{1, \dots, m\}$.

  Problem \eqref{eq:B} would typically be associated with an original problem
  \begin{equation}
    \min \{ d(x) + c(y) : g \le Fx + E(y) \le h, x \in X, y \in Y \} \tag{$O$}
    \label{eq:O}
  \end{equation}
  defined in terms of variables $x$ and $y$. By reformulating problem \eqref{eq:O} as
  \begin{equation}
    \min \{ d(x) + \phi(x) : x \in X \}, \tag{$O'$}
    \label{eq:O_prime}
  \end{equation}
  where
  \begin{equation}
    \phi(x) = \min \{ c(y) : (g - Fx) \le E(y) \le (h - Fx), y \in Y \}, \tag{$P$}
    \label{eq:P}
  \end{equation}
  we see that \eqref{eq:P} assumes the form of \eqref{eq:B} with $w = g - Fx$
  and $z = h - Fx$. The \code{BendersBFunction} represents the function $\phi$
  whose underlying optimization problem is given by \eqref{eq:B}. The
  variables $x$ are the active \code{Variable}s of this
  \code{BendersBFunction}. As can be seen in \eqref{eq:P}, the left- and
  right-hand sides of (some or all) the constraints may depend on $x$. The
  \Block $B$, however, does not depend on $x$. In order to have the left- and
  right-hand sides of the constraints in \eqref{eq:B} dependent on $x$, we
  consider the mappings $x \rightarrow g - Fx$ and $x \rightarrow h - Fx$,
  which are used to update the left- and right-hand sides $w$ and $z$ of the
  constraints in \eqref{eq:B} according to the values of the variables $x$.

  These mappings are bunched together into a single mapping $M$ defined by the
  matrix $A$ and the vector b such that
  \begin{equation}
    M(x) = Ax + b.
    \label{eq:benders_mapping}
  \end{equation}
  The $i$-th component
  of this mapping, $M_i(x) = [ Ax + b ]_i$, is associated with the left- or
  right-hand side (or both) of the \code{RowConstraint} of \Block $B$ pointed
  by $C_i$. The \code{ConstraintSide} $S_i$ indicates which sides of the
  \code{RowConstraint} (pointed by) $C_i$ are affected, as follows:

  \begin{itemize}
  \item If $S_i = $ `L', then $M_i(x)$ gives the value of the left-hand
    side of the \code{RowConstraint} (pointed by) $C_i$.

  \item If $S_i = $ `R', then $M_i(x)$ gives the value of the
    right-hand side of the \code{RowConstraint} (pointed by) $C_i$.

  \item If $S_i = $ `B', then $M_i(x)$ gives the value of both the
    left- and right-hand side of the \code{RowConstraint} (pointed by)
    $C_i$. This is the case, for example, of an equality constraint, in which
    the left- and right-hand sides are equal.
  \end{itemize}

  Notice that the affected constraints must necessarily be
  \code{RowConstraint}. Moreover, if a \code{RowConstraint} in \Block $B$ has
  finite bounds that can be different from each other and are affected by the
  values of $x$, then it would be listed twice (once for each of its
  bounds). That is, there would be indices $i, j \in I$ such that $i \ne j$
  and $C_i = C_j$, $S_i = $ `L', and $S_j = $ `R'.

  \note[Important:]{The vector of pairs $[ ( C_i , S_i ) ]_{i \in I}$ must
    not contain duplicate entries (i.e., there must not exist indices $i, j
    \in I$ such that $i \ne j$, $C_i = C_j$ and $S_i = S_j$). Moreover, if
    there is $i \in I$ such that $S_i = $ `B', there must not exist
    $j \in I$ such that $C_i = C_j$.}

  We stress that the $i$-th active \Variable of this \code{BendersBFunction}
  is associated with the $i$-th column of the mapping matrix $A$. In other
  words, the $i$-th active \Variable serves as the coefficient of the $i$-th
  column of the $A$ matrix in the mapping $M$.
\end{enumerate}

Note that the \code{BendersBFunction} is not supposed to have any \Variable or
\Constraint (besides those defined in its only inner \Block $B$).

An \SMSpp \netCDF \group for a \code{BendersBFunction} contains the following:
\begin{itemize}
\item The \dimension \cstr{NumVar} containing the number of columns of the $A$
  matrix, i.e., the number of active variables.

\item The \dimension \cstr{NumRow} containing the number of rows of the $A$
  matrix. This \dimension is optional; if it is not provided then 0 (no
  rows) is assumed.

\item The \dimension \cstr{NumNonzero}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint},
  containing the number of nonzero entries in the $A$ matrix. This \dimension
  is optional and determines in which format the matrix $A$ is given. If this
  \dimension is present, then \cstr{NumRow} must also be. If \cstr{NumNonzero}
  is not present, then the $A$ matrix is given as a dense matrix. If it is
  present, then the $A$ matrix is given in a sparse format as defined by the
  \variables \cstr{Row}, \cstr{Column}, and \cstr{A}. During serialization, the
  following criterion is used to decide the format in which the $A$ matrix is
  stored. If at most 25\% of its elements are nonzero, it is stored in sparse
  format; otherwise, it is stored in dense format.

\item The \variable \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint}
  and indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NumRow}, containing the number of
  nonzero elements of the $A$ matrix in each row.  If the \dimension
  \cstr{NumNonzero} is not present, then this \variable is ignored.  If
  \cstr{NumNonzero} is present, then \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} is optional only
  if \cstr{NumVar}, \cstr{NumRow}, and \cstr{NumNonzero} are equal. In this
  case, if \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} is not provided, then the $A$ matrix is
  assumed to be the identity matrix. If \cstr{NumVar}, \cstr{NumRow}, and
  \cstr{NumNonzero} are not all equal, then \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} must be
  provided. For each $i \in \{0, \dots, \code{NumRow}-1\}$,
  \code{NumNonzeroAtRow[i]} is the number of nonzero elements in the $i$-th
  row of the $A$ matrix.

\item The \variable \cstr{Column}, of type \code{netCDF::NcUint} and indexed
  over the \dimension \cstr{NumNonzero}, containing the column indices of the
  entries of the matrix. If \cstr{NumNonzero} or \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} is not
  present, then this \variable is ignored. For each $k \in \{0, \dots,
  \code{NumNonzero}-1\}$, \code{Column[k]} is the column index of the k-th
  nonzero entry of the $A$ matrix, whose value is given by
  \code{A[k]}. \cstr{Column} stores the column indices in row-major order,
  that is, if $(i,j)$ and $(p,q)$ are the entries of the $k$-th and $l$-th
  nonzero elements of $A$, respectively, with $k < l$, then $i \le p$.

\item The \variable \cstr{A}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble}. This \variable
  stores the values of the elements of the $A$ matrix of the mapping. If the
  \dimension \cstr{NumNonzero} is provided but the \variable
  \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} is not, then the $A$ matrix is assumed to be the
  identity matrix and the \variable \cstr{A} is ignored. If both the
  \dimension \cstr{NumNonzero} and the \variable \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} are
  provided, then the \cstr{A} \variable is indexed over the \cstr{NumNonzero}
  \dimension and contains the values of the (potentially) nonzero entries of
  the matrix. In this case, \code{A[k]} is the value of the $k$-th nonzero
  entry, whose column index is given by \code{Column[k]}. The nonzero elements
  of the matrix are given in left-to-right top-to-bottom (``row-major'')
  order. If the \dimension \cstr{NumNonzero} is not present, then \cstr{A} is
  indexed over both the \cstr{NumRow} and \cstr{NumVar} \dimensions (in this
  order); it contains the (row-major) representation of the matrix $A$. This
  \variable is optional only if \code{NumRow == 0}.

\item The \variable \cstr{b}, of type \code{netCDF::NcDouble} and indexed over
  the \dimension \cstr{NumRow}, which contains the vector $b$. This \variable is
  optional. If it is not provided, then we assume \code{b[i] = 0} for each $i
  \in \{0, \dots, \cstr{NumRow} - 1\}$.

\item The \variable \cstr{ConstraintSide}, of type \code{netCDF::NcChar} and
  indexed over the \dimension \cstr{NumRow}, indicating, at position $i$, which
  side of the $i$-th \Constraint is affected. The possible values are `L' for
  the left-hand (or lower bound) side, `R' for the right-hand (or upper bound)
  side, and `B' for both sides. This \variable is optional. If it is not
  provided, then all entries of this array are assumed to be `B'.

\item The \group \cstr{AbstractPath} containing the description of a vector of
  \code{AbstractPath} to the affected \RowConstraints. The number of
  \code{AbstractPath} in that vector must be equal to the number of rows of
  the $A$ matrix and, therefore, the \dimension associated with the number of
  \code{AbstractPath} is \cstr{NumRow}. The $i$-th \code{AbstractPath} in this
  vector must be the path to the $i$-th affected \code{RowConstraint} (which
  is associated with the $i$-th row of the $A$ matrix). This \group is
  optional only if \code{NumRow == 0}. Each \code{AbstractPath} is taken with
  respect to the inner \Block of this \code{BendersBFunction} (i.e., the inner
  \Block of this \code{BendersBFunction} must be the reference \Block of the
  path).

\item The \group \cstr{Block}, containing the description of the inner
  \Block.

\item The \group \cstr{BlockConfig}, containing the \BlockConfig of the inner
  \Block. This \group is optional.

\item The \group \cstr{BlockSolver}, containing the \BlockSolverConfig of the
  inner \Block.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{\BlockConfig for existing \dBlock}

As described in \S \ref{sec:BlockConfig}, each \Block can have several \Configuration parameters, which can be dealt with by a \BlockConfig object. It makes therefore sense to collect here all possible \Configuration parameters for existing \dBlock, since these may end up in a \SMSpp \netCDF file.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Parameters of \code{MCFBlock}}

The \code{MCFBlock} class defines the following \Configuration parameters:
%
\begin{itemize}
 \item \code{f\_static\_constraints\_Configuration}. The static constraint of a MCF are the flow conservation equations and the bound constraints. The latter are typically implemented via a \code{std::vector<LB0Constraint>} with exactly $m$ entries. However, If \emph{all} the upper bound are $+\infty$, it is possible to skip the \code{std::vector< LB0Constraint >} entirely and just use the fact that the \code{ColVariable can be defined to be non-negative}. If \code{f\_static\_constraints\_Configuration} is set, it is a \code{SimpleConfiguration<int>} whose \code{f\_value} is not $0$, the bound constraints are not implemented if it is possible to do so; otherwise they are always implemented. Note that this makes it impossible to change any upper bound.
 %
 \item \code{f\_is\_feasible\_Configuration}. If \code{f\_is\_feasible\_Configuration} is a \code{SimpleConfiguration< double >}, its \code{f\_value} is taken as the parameter for deciding what ``approximately feasible'' exactly means while checking satisfaction of both flow conservation constraint and flow upper/lower bounds; the value is taken as the \emph{relative} tolerance for those checks.
 %
 \item \code{f\_is\_optimal\_Configuration}. Checking optimality of a primal-dual solution of the MCF problems means checking that both are approximately feasible, and that they approximately satisfy the Complementary Slackness Conditions. This requires two parameters for deciding what ``approximately feasible'' means, one for the primal ($\epsilon_f$) and one for the dual ($\epsilon_c$). If \code{f\_is\_optimal\_Configuration} is a \code{SimpleConfiguration< std::pair<double,double> >}, then $\epsilon_c =$ \code{f\_value.first} and $\epsilon_f =$ \code{f\_value.second}.
 %
 \item \code{f\_solution\_Configuration}. For all the methods dealing with solutions, i.e., \code{get\_Solution()} and \code{map\_\-[back/\-forward]\_solution()}, if \code{f\_solution\_Configuration} is a \code{SimpleConfiguration<int>}, then its \code{f\_value} decides what is mapped, with the following encoding: $1$ means ``only map the primal solution'', $2$ means ``only map the dual solution'', everything else (e.g., $0$) means
 ``map everything''.
\end{itemize}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{\ComputeConfig for existing \dSolver}

As described in \S \ref{sec:ComputeConfig}, each \Block can have several \ComputeConfig parameters, which can be dealt with by a \BlockSolverConfig object. It makes therefore sense to collect here all possible \ComputeConfig parameters for the existing objects that define actual values for their algorithmic parameters, since these may end up in a \SMSpp \netCDF file.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Standard parameters in \Solver}

The base \Solver class defines a set of ``general'' algorithmic parameters, described in the following table.

\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{13.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intMaxIter} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum number of iterations that the next call to \code{solve()} is allowed to execute for trying to solve the \Block. The concept of ``what exactly an iteration is'' is clearly \Solver-dependent, and the user of the \Solver need supposedly be aware of which concrete \dSolver it is actually using to be able to sensibly set this parameter; however, because most \Solver will actually be iterative processes, it makes sense to offer  support for this notion in the base class. \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intMaxSol} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum  number of different solutions to the \Block that the \Solver should attempt to obtain and store. Mathematical models can have (very) many solutions: an objective function precisely helps in selecting among them, but even that may not be enough to narrow the choice down to a single solution (multiple optima may exist, or quasi-optimal solutions may also be sought for for any number of reasons). On the other hand, a solution can be a ``big'' object, and storing it may be costly. It may be therefore helpful for a Solver to know beforehand how many different solutions the user would like to get. Among the reasonable values for this parameter, $1$ says ``I don't care of multiple solutions, give me only the best one'', and $0$ says ``I don't care of solutions at all, just tell me if there is any, and what its value is''. The default is $1$. \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intLogVerb} & \code{int} & An integer parameter dictating how ``verbose'' the log of the \Solver has to be. The specific meaning of each value is \Solver-dependent, but it is intended that $0$ means ``no log at all'', and increasing values correspond to increasing verbosity. The default value is $0$ (no log). \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblMaxTime} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum time limit that the next call to \code{solve()} can expend in trying to solve the \Block. The value is assumed to be in seconds, and it's a double (so both very quick and very slow solvers are supported). The base \Solver class does not explicitly distinguish between ``wall-clock time'' and ``CPU time'', which may be rather different especially in a parallel environment, but this concept can be easily added by derived classes.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblRelAcc} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the \emph{relative} accuracy required to the solution of the Block. This is geared towards single-objective optimization problems, and it is defined as follows: a solution for a \emph{minimization} problem has a relative accuracy of $\varepsilon$ if a feasible (to within the required tolerance for each \Block) solution has been obtained, an upper bound $ub$ on its objective function value has been found, a lower bound $lb$ on the optimal value of the problem has been found, and $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon \max( |lb| , 1 )$. The roles of $ub$ and $lb$ are suitably reversed for a maximization problem. The default is \code{1e-6}.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblAbsAcc} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the \emph{absolute} accuracy required to the solution of the model. This is geared towards single-objective optimization problems, and it is defined as follows: a solution for a \emph{minimization} problem has a absolute accuracy of $\varepsilon$ if a feasible  (to within the required tolerance for each \Block) solution has been obtained, an upper bound $ub$ on its objective function value has been found, a lower bound $lb$ on the optimal value of the problem has been found, and $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon$. The roles of $ub$ and $lb$ are suitably reversed for a maximization problem. The default is $+\infty$, which is intended to mean that the only working accuracy is the relative one.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblUpCutOff} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the \emph{upper cut off} of the solution process. This is a value basically telling the \Solver ``when enough is enough''. In particular, if the Solver obtains a certified lower bound $lb$ on the optimal value such that $lb \geq \varepsilon$ with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$, then it can stop. This is a certificate that the optimal value is at least $\varepsilon$. For a maximization problem the condition says: I actually needed a solution with objective function value at least as good as (i.e., larger than) $\varepsilon$, now that I have found one the problem is as good as solved to me. Hence this parameter is analogous to the maximum absolute accuracy \code{dblAbsAcc}, but ``weaker'' in that it does not not require any upper bound to work. For a minimization problem, instead, the condition says: I actually needed a solution with objective function value at least as good as (i.e., smaller than) $\varepsilon$, now that I have know for sure that this is never going to happen the problem is as good as unfeasible to me. The default is $+\infty$.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblLwCutOff} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the \emph{lower cut off} of the solution process. This is a value basically telling the \Solver ``when enough is enough''. In particular, if the \Solver obtains a certified upper bound $ub$ on the optimal value such that $ub \leq \varepsilon$ with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$, then it can stop. This is a certificate that the optimal value is at most $\varepsilon$. For a minimization problem the condition says: I actually needed a solution with objective function value at least as good as (i.e., smaller than) $\varepsilon$, now that I have found one the problem is as good as solved to me. Hence this parameter is analogous to the maximum absolute accuracy \code{dblAbsAcc}, but ``weaker'' in that it does not not require any lower bound to work. For a maximization problem, instead, the condition says: I actually needed a solution with objective function value at least as good as (i.e., larger than) $\varepsilon$, now that I have know for sure that this is never going to happen the problem is as good as unfeasible to me. The default is $-\infty$.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblRAccSol} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the relative accuracy of the accepted solutions. It instructs the \Solver not to even consider a solution among the ones to be reported \code{intMaxSol} if its objective function value is ``too'' bad. For a minimization problem, the objective function value of a feasible solution provides an upper bound $ub$ on the optimal value. Assuming a lower bound $lb$ on the optimal value of the problem has been found, a solution is deemed acceptable with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$ if $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon \max( |ub| , |lb| , 1 )$. Note that if no $lb$ is available, the above formula can be replaced with $ub - f_{best} \leq \varepsilon \max( |ub| , |f_{best}| , 1 )$, where $f_{best}$ is the value of the best (with smallest objective value) solution found so far. The roles of $ub$ and $lb$ are suitably reversed for a maximization problem. The default is $+\infty$.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblAAccSol} & \code{dbl} & Similar to \code{dblRAccSol} but for an \emph{absolute} accuracy; that is, a solution is deemed acceptable with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$ if $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon$ or $ub - f_{best} \leq \varepsilon$ with the same notation as in \code{dblRAccSol} and the same provisions about the case of a maximization problem. The default is $+\infty$.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblFAccSol} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum relative allowed violation of constraints. Whenever the \Solver is incapable of finding feasible solutions (maybe because there is none), it may still be useful that it returns the ``least unfeasible'' ones. This parameter instructs the \Solver not to even consider a solution among the ones to be reported (see \code{intMaxSol}) if its violation is ``too'' bad. The actual meaning of this parameter may be \Solver-dependent, but it can be thought to work as the ``relative constraint violation''. A setting of $0$ may be taken as a way to tell the Solver not to bother to produce unfeasible solutions at all, which is why this is the default value of the parameter.\\
  \hline
\end{longtable}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Standard parameters in \code{CDASolver}}

The \code{CDASolver} class extends the parameters of \Solver with the ones described  in the following table.

\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{13.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intMaxDSol} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum number of different \emph{dual} solutions that the \Solver should attempt to obtain and store. Since dual solutions can be a ``big'' objects (in some cases, much bigger than primal ones), storing them may be costly. It may be therefore helpful for a \code{CDASolver} to know beforehand how many different dual solutions the user would like to get. Among the reasonable values for this parameter, $1$ says ``I don't care of multiple dual solutions, give me only the best one'', and $0$ says ``I don't care of dual solutions at all, just tell me if there is any, and what its value is''. The default is $1$. \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblRAccDSol} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the relative accuracy of the accepted \emph{dual} solutions. It instructs the \code{CDASolver} not to even consider a solution among the ones to be reported (see \code{intMaxDSol}) if its objective function value is ``too'' bad. If the original problem is a minimization one, its dual is a maximization one. Hence, the objective function value of a dual solution provides a lower bound $lb$ on the optimal dual value (which may, or may not, be equal to that of the primal problem). Assuming an upper bound $ub$ on the optimal value of the \emph{dual} problem has been found (note that this can be obtained by means of the objective value of a feasible \emph{primal} solution), a solution is deemed acceptable with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$ if $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon \max( |ub| , |lb| , 1 )$. Note that if no $ub$ is available, the above formula can be replaced with $f_{best} - lb \leq \varepsilon \max( |f_{best}| , |lb| , 1 )$ where $f_{best}$ is the value of the best (with largest objective value) solution found so far. The roles of $ub$ and $lb$ are suitably reversed if the primal is a maximization problem, so that the dual is a minimization one. The default is $+\infty$. \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblAAccDSol} & \code{dbl} & Similar to \code{dblRAccDSol} but for an \emph{absolute} accuracy; that is, a dual solution is deemed acceptable with the provided parameter $\varepsilon$ if $ub - lb \leq \varepsilon$ or $f_{best} - lb \leq \varepsilon$ with the same notation as in \code{dblRAccDSol} and the same provisions about the case of a maximization problem. The default is $+\infty$. \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblFAccDSol} & \code{dbl} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum relative allowed violation of \emph{dual} constraints, assuming of course something like that exists in the specific dual that is being dealt with. Whenever the \code{CDASolver} is incapable of finding feasible dual solutions (maybe because there is none), it may still be useful that it returns the ``least unfeasible'' ones. This parameter instructs the \code{CDASolver} not to even consider a solution among the ones to be reported (see \code{intMaxDSol}) if its violation is ``too'' bad. The actual meaning of this parameter is necessarily be \code{CDASolver}-dependent; intuitively, it may be thought to work as the ``relative constraint violation'' \code{feas\_epsilon} of \Block if the concept of ``dual constraint'' is applicable. A setting of $0$ may be taken as a way to tell the \code{CDASolver} not to bother to produce unfeasible solutions at all, which is why this is the default value of the parameter. \\
 %
 \hline
\end{longtable}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Standard parameters in \code{Function}}

The base \code{Function} class defines a set of ``general'' algorithmic parameters, described in the following table.

\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{13.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intMaxIter} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the maximum number of iterations in the next call to \code{compute()}. The concept of ``what exactly an iteration is'' is clearly dependent on the \code{Function}, and it may not even make sense for all \code{Function}. However, some \code{Function} will actually be iterative processes, and therefore it makes sense to offer support for this notion in the base class. The default is $+\infty =$ no limits.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblMaxTime} & \code{dbl} & The parameter for setting the maximum time limit for the next call to \code{compute()}. The value is assumed to be in seconds, and it's a double (so both very fast and very slow computations are supported). The \code{Function} class (so far) does not explicitly distinguish between ``wall-clock time'' and ``CPU time'', which may be rather different especially in a parallel environment. The default is $+\infty$.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblRelAcc} & \code{dbl} & The parameter for setting the \emph{relative} accuracy required to the function value. That is, if both an upper bound $ub$ and a lower bound $lb$ on the value have been found, then \code{compute()} can stop as soon as $ub - lb \leq \code{dblRelAcc} \max( |lb| , 1 )$. The default is \code{1e-6}.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblAbsAcc} & \code{dbl} & The parameter for setting the \emph{absolute} accuracy required to the function value. That is, if both an upper bound $ub$ and a lower bound $lb$ on the value have been found, then \code{compute()} can stop as soon as $ub - lb \leq \code{dblRelAcc}$. The default is $+\infty$, which is intended to mean that the only working accuracy is the relative one.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblUpCutOff} & \code{dbl} & The parameter for setting the ``upper cut off'' of the computation; that is, if a lower bound $lb$ on the value has been found, then \code{compute()} can stop as soon as $lb \geq \code{dblUpCutOff}$. This is a certificate that the value is at least \code{dblUpCutOff}. The default is $+\infty$, i.e., no upper cut off.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblLwCutOff} & \code{dbl} & The parameter for setting the ``lower cut off'' of the computation; that is, if an upper bound $ub$ on the value has been found, then \code{compute()} can stop as soon as $ub \leq \code{dblLwCutOff}$. This is a certificate that the value is at most \code{dblLwCutOff}. The default is $-\infty$, i.e., no lower cut off.\\
 \hline
\end{longtable}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Standard parameters in \code{C05Function}}

The \code{C05Function} class extends the parameters of \code{Function} with the ones described in the following table.

\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{13.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intLPMaxSz} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the size of the ``local pool'', that is, the maximum number of linearizations that should be stored in the local pool. The default is $1$, which corresponds to the fact that the \code{Function} can only produce a single linearization at a time (for it is, say, smooth).\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{intGPMaxSz} & \code{int} & The algorithmic parameter for setting the size of the ``global pool'', that is, the maximum number of linearizations that should be stored in the local pool. The default is $0$, which corresponds to the fact that the \code{Function} cannot store any linearization (for it is, say, smooth and therefore there is no need to).\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblRAccLin} & \code{dbl} & A linearization $( g , \alpha )$ computed at the point $x$ is ``accurate'' if the value of the linearization coincides with the value of the function at $x$, i.e., $\alpha = f(x)$. In general linearizations that are not ``completely accurate'' can still be useful: for instance, in the Lagrangian case an $\varepsilon$-optimal solution to the Lagrangian problem gives rise to a valid linearization $( g , \alpha )$ with $\varepsilon \geq f(x) - \alpha$. This can be deemed interesting if $\varepsilon$ is ``small'', but not if $\varepsilon$ is ``large''. This parameter instructs the \code{C05Function} not to bother reporting (and therefore storing in the ``local pool'') any linearization having a relative error with $f(x)$ larger than \code{dblRAccLin}. This would generally mean $| f(x) - \alpha | \leq \code{dblRAccLin}  \max( | f(x) | , | \alpha | , 1 )$, except that the value $f(x)$ may not be known exactly, with only lower and/or upper bounds on it available. The actual formula therefore depends on what information is actually available: for instance, in the Lagrangian case one knows that $f(x) \geq \alpha$, and therefore typically an upper estimate $ub \geq f(x)$ is used in the formula instead of $f(x)$. The default is $0$, i.e., ``only perfect linearizations are allowed''.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{dblAAccLin} & \code{dbl} &  Similar to \code{dblRAccLin} but for an \emph{absolute} accuracy; that is, a linearization is deemed acceptable if $| f(x) - \alpha | \leq \code{dblAAccLin}$, except that the value $f(x)$ may not be known exactly, with only lower and/or upper bounds on it available. The actual formula therefore depends on what information is actually available: for instance, in the Lagrangian case one knows that $f(x) \geq \alpha$, and therefore typically an upper estimate $ub \geq f(x)$ is used in the formula instead of $f(x)$. The default is $0$, i.e., ``only perfect linearizations are allowed''. \\
 \hline
\end{longtable}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection{Parameters of \code{MCFSolver<MCFC>}}

The \code{MCFSolver<>} class derives from \code{CDASolver}, and therefore it extends the parameters of with the ones described in the following table.


\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{14.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{kReopt} & \code{int} & Whether the MCF algorithm (whatever it is) should re-optimize after changes in the data, as opposed to restarting from scratch.\\
 \hline
\end{longtable}

However, \code{MCFSolver<>} is a template class, whose template parameter actually is a concrete MCF solver object derived from \code{MCFClass}. Each of these has its own algorithmic parameters, which are described in the next sub-sections.

%-  -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -   -
\subsubsection{Parameters of \code{MCFSolver<MCFSimplex>}}


\begin{longtable}{|c|c|p{13.5cm}|}
 \hline
 name & type & description \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{kAlgPrimal} & \code{int} & If $> 0$ it instructs the \code{MCFSimplex} solver to use the primal (network) simplex method, otherwise the dual one.\\
 \hline
 \code{kAlgPricing} & \code{int} & Selects which pricing rule is used within the algorithm. Possible values are \code{kDantzig} $= 0$ (Dantzig's rule, most violated constraint), \code{kFirstEligibleArc} $= 1$ (first eligible arc in round-robin), and \code{kCandidateListPivot} $= 2$ (Candidate List Pivot Rule). \\
 %
 \hline
 \code{kNumCandList} & \code{int} & Parameter to set the number of candidate list for the Candidate List Pivot method.\\
 %
 \hline
 \code{kHotListSize} & \code{int} & Parameter to set the size of Hot List for the Candidate List Pivot method.\\
 \hline
\end{longtable}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{\ComputeConfig for existing \dFunction}

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\subsection {Parameters of \code{BendersBFunction}}

The \code{BendersBFunction} class extends the parameters of \code{C05Function}
with the ones described in the following table. Setting any of these
parameters causes the corresponding parameter of the \code{CDASolver} of the
inner \Block of the \code{BendersBFunction} to be overwritten (if the
\code{BendersBFunction} has an inner \Block and this \Block has a
\code{CDASolver} attached to it).\\
