%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{Examples}

\subsection{Seasonal Storage Valuation}\label{sec:SSV}

The multistage stochastic problem in the Seasonal Storage Valuation (SSV) is
represented by an \code{SDDPBlock} (see \S \ref{sec:SDDPBlock}). The
\code{SDDPBlock} has $T$ nested \Blocks, where $T$ is the time horizon and,
therefore, we have $\code{TimeHorizon} = T$ in the specification of
\code{SDDPBlock}. For each $t \in \{0, \dots,T-1\}$, the $t$-th nested \Block
of the \code{SDDPBlock} is a \code{StochasticBlock} (see \S
\ref{sec:StochasticBlock}) which is associated with stage $t$. The
\cstr{TimeHorizon} of an \code{SDDPBlock} could be, for instance, 52, the
number of weeks of a year. Then, each sub-\Block of this \code{SDDPBlock}
could be associated with a particular week of the year.

\begin{figure}[h!]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[scale=1.5]{img/sddp.pdf}
  \caption{An \code{SDDPBlock} and its nested {\Block}s.}
\end{figure}

The set of scenarios in \code{SDDPBlock} is given by a matrix (see the
\variable \cstr{Scenarios} in \S \ref{sec:SDDPBlock}), in which each row
represents a scenario that spans the time horizon. If there are $m$ scenarios
(see \dimension \cstr{NumberScenarios} in \S \ref{sec:SDDPBlock}) and each
scenario is a (row) vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$ (in which case $n$ would be the
size of a scenario; see \dimension \cstr{ScenarioSize} in \S
\ref{sec:SDDPBlock}), a matrix $\mathcal{S} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with
scenarios would have the form
\[
\mathcal{S} = \left[
\begin{array}{cccc}
  s_{0,0}   & s_{0,1}   & \dots  & s_{0, n-1}\\
  s_{1,0}   & s_{1,1}   & \dots  & s_{1, n-1}\\
  \vdots    & \vdots    & \vdots & \vdots \\
  s_{m-1,0} & s_{m-1,1} & \dots  & s_{m-1, n-1}\\
\end{array}
\right].
\]
For each $i \in \{ 0, \dots, m-1 \}$, the $i$-th scenario, represented by the vector
\[
S_i = \left( s_{i,0},  s_{i,1} , \dots , s_{i, n-1} \right),
\]
is assumed to be organized in such a way that
\[
S_i = \left( S_{i,0},  S_{i,1} , \dots , S_{i, T-1} \right),
\]
where $S_{i,t}$, the $t$-th sub-scenario of $S_i$, contains the data for the
sub-problem at stage $t$ (which is represented by the $t$-th
\code{StochasticBlock} of the \code{SDDPBlock}). The sub-scenarios for
different stages may have different sizes. For each $i \in \{ 0, \dots, m-1 \}$
and $t \in \{0, \dots, T-1\}$, $S_{i,t} \in \mathbb{R}^{n_t}$, so that
\[
n = \sum_{t = 0}^{T-1} n_t.
\]
In the specification of \code{SDDPBlock}, the vector $( n_0, \dots, n_{T-1})$
containing the sizes of the sub-scenarios is given by the \variable
\cstr{SubScenarioSize}. This \variable is optional and, in the case it is not
provided, all sub-scenarios are assumed to have the same size, i.e., $n_t = n
/ T$ for each $t \in \{0, \dots, T-1\}$, and $n$ is a multiple of $T$.

If all sub-scenarios have the same size, their data must be organized so that
related random data are in contiguous parts of the vector representing a
sub-scenario. For instance, suppose that each sub-scenario contains data
related to demand, inflow, and wind power. In this example, we would say that
there are three groups of data (the \dimension \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups}
in \code{SDDPBlock} would then be 3) and, for each $i \in \{ 0, \dots, m-1 \}$
and $t \in \{0, \dots, T-1\}$, the sub-scenario $S_{i,t}$ would be written as
\[
S_{i,t} = \left( D_{i,t} , F_{i,t} , W_{i,t} \right).
\]
The \variable \cstr{SizeRandomDataGroups} of \code{SDDPBlock} would then be an
array containing the sizes of the sub-vectors $D_{i,t}$, $F_{i,t}$, and
$W_{i,t}$. For instance, if $D_{i,t}$ is a vector with size 7, $F_{i,t}$ is
one with size 4, and $W_{i,t}$ is one with size 11, the \variable
\cstr{SizeRandomDataGroups} would contain the array $(7, 4, 11)$. Hence, the
matrix $\mathcal{S}$ would then have the following form:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    label distance=10pt
  ]

\tikzset{
  s/.style={
    above left offset={-0.2,0.45},
    below right offset={0.2,-0.3},
    set fill color=gray!5,
    set border color=gray!20,
  },
  sin/.style={
    set fill color=gray!20,
    set border color=gray!60,
}}

\matrix [matrix of math nodes,left delimiter={[},right delimiter={]},row sep=0.1cm,column sep=0.1cm] (m) {
\tikzmarkin[s]{S0} \tikzmarkin[sin]{S0in}D_{0,0}   & F_{0,0}   & W_{0,0}\tikzmarkend{S0in}   &  \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{S0int}D_{0,t}   & F_{0,t}   & W_{0,t}\tikzmarkend{S0int}   & \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{S0inT}D_{0,T-1}   & F_{0,T-1}   & W_{0,T-1}\tikzmarkend{S0inT}\tikzmarkend{S0}\\
                   \vdots    & \vdots    & \vdots    &  \cdots  & \vdots    & \vdots    & \vdots    & \cdots  & \vdots      & \vdots      & \vdots\\
\tikzmarkin[s]{S1} \tikzmarkin[sin]{S1in}D_{i,0}   & F_{i,0}   & W_{i,0}\tikzmarkend{S1in}   &  \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{S1int}D_{i,t}   & F_{i,t}   & W_{i,t}\tikzmarkend{S1int}   & \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{S1inT}D_{i,T-1}   & F_{i,T-1}   & W_{i,T-1}\tikzmarkend{S1inT}\tikzmarkend{S1}\\
                   \vdots    & \vdots    & \vdots    &  \cdots  & \vdots    & \vdots    & \vdots    & \cdots  & \vdots      & \vdots      & \vdots\\
\tikzmarkin[s]{Sm} \tikzmarkin[sin]{Smin}D_{m-1,0} & F_{m-1,0} & W_{m-1,0}\tikzmarkend{Smin} &  \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{Smint}D_{m-1,t} & F_{m-1,t} & W_{m-1,t}\tikzmarkend{Smint} & \cdots  & \tikzmarkin[sin]{SminT}D_{m-1,T-1} & F_{m-1,T-1} & W_{m-1,T-1}\tikzmarkend{SminT}\tikzmarkend{Sm}\\
};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-1)(m-1-3),
  inner xsep=0,
  above delimiter=\{,
  label=above:Stage $0$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-5)(m-1-7),
  inner xsep=0,
  above delimiter=\{,
  label=above:Stage $t$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-4),
  label=above:$\cdots$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-8),
  label=above:$\cdots$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-9)(m-1-11),
  inner xsep=0,
  above delimiter=\{,
  label=above:Stage $T-1$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-11.center),
  inner xsep=25pt,inner ysep=0,
  label=right:Scenario $0$,
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-2-11),
  inner xsep=40pt,inner ysep=0,
  label=right:$\vdots$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-3-11.center),
  inner xsep=25pt,inner ysep=0,
  label=right:Scenario $i$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-4-11),
  inner xsep=40pt,inner ysep=0,
  label=right:$\vdots$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-5-11.center),
  inner xsep=25pt,inner ysep=0,
  label=right:Scenario $m-1$
] {};

\end{tikzpicture}
\end{equation*}
In this example, if $T=52$, the \dimension \cstr{ScenarioSize} would be
$52(7+4+11)$. The light gray box indicate a scenario while a dark gray box
indicates a sub-scenario.

Each sub-\Block of the \code{SDDPBlock} represents a short-term problem that
is associated with a \code{UCBlock} as follows. For each $t \in \{0, \dots,
T-1\}$, the inner \Block of the $t$-th \code{StochasticBlock} of the
\code{SDDPBlock} is a \code{BendersBlock}. The \code{BendersBlock} has a set
of {\Variable}s and an \Objective. Its \Objective is an \code{FRealObjective}
whose \Function is a \code{BendersBFunction}. The {\Variable}s of the
\code{BendersBlock} are the ones that are active in the
\code{BendersBFunction}. The inner \Block of the \code{BendersBFunction} is a
\code{UCBlock} (or a Lagrangian relaxation of a \code{UCBlock}) which depends
on the values of the {\Variable}s that are active in the
\code{BendersBFunction}. In the SSV, the {\Variable}s defined in the
\code{BendersBlock} (which are active in the \code{BendersBFunction})
represent the initial volumes of the reservoirs. These affect the values of
the left- or right-hand side of some {\Constraint}s of the \code{UCBlock}. The
affected {\Constraint}s and how they are affected are determined by the
\code{BendersBFunction}. In the \code{BendersBFunction}, the affected
{\Constraint}s are indicated by a vector of \code{AbstractPath} (each
path being relative to the \code{BendersBlock}) and the affected sides of
the {\Constraint}s are indicated by the one-dimensional \variable
\cstr{ConstraintSide}.
The way the {\Constraint}s are affected by those {\Variable}s is defined by
the linear mapping given by the \netCDF \variables \cstr{A} and \cstr{b}
(together with their auxiliary \variables and \dimensions).
Figure~\ref{fig:stochastic_block} summarizes the structure of an
\code{StochasticBlock} in which $x_t$ represent the \Variables of the
\code{BendersBlock}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[scale=1.5]{img/stochastic_block.pdf}
  \caption{Structure of an \code{StochasticBlock} in the SSV.}
  \label{fig:stochastic_block}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{\code{BendersBlock}}\label{sec:ssv_benders_block}

Before giving an example on how to construct the \code{BendersBlock}, we shall
look into some details of the \code{UCBlock}. Following the example above, in
which the \code{SDDPBlock} represents a multistage problem over 52 weeks,
there would be one \code{UCBlock} associated with each of those 52 weeks. Each
\code{UCBlock} could be, for instance, a deterministic multistage problem with
168 stages (one stage for each hour of the week). In this case, the dimension
\code{TimeHorizon} in the specification of the \code{UCBlock} would be 168. As
you can see, there are two independent time horizons: one at the level of the
\code{SDDPBlock} (the mid-term problem) and one at the level of the
\code{UCBlock} (the short-term problem). To avoid confusion, we will refer to
the time horizon and stages of the \code{SDDPBlock} as the outer time horizon
and outer stages, respectively, and the time horizon and stages of the
\code{UCBlock} as the inner time horizon and inner stages, respectively. We
will also use the notations $T^{\text{out}}$ and $T^{\text{in}}$ to refer to
the outer and inner time horizons when necessary.

A \code{UCBlock} has two sets of sub-{\Block}s. The first one is a set of
\code{UnitBlock}s whose size is given by the \cstr{NumberUnits}
\dimension. The second one is a set of \code{NetworkBlock}s whose size is
(inner) \cstr{TimeHorizon}. Figure~\ref{fig:ucblock} illustrates the
\code{UCBlock} and its nested {\Block}s. In this figure, $N$ is the number of
\code{UnitBlock}s (\dimension \cstr{NumberUnits}) and $T^{\text{in}}$ is the
inner time horizon (\dimension \cstr{TimeHorizon} in the specification of
\code{UCBlock}).

\begin{figure}[h!]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[scale=1.5]{img/ucblock.pdf}
  \caption{\code{UCBlock} and its nested {\Block}s.}
  \label{fig:ucblock}
\end{figure}

There can be many types of \code{UnitBlock}s in the \code{UCBlock} (e.g.,
\code{ThermalUnitBlock}, \code{BatteryUnitBlock}, \dots). In the SSV, exactly
one of those \code{UnitBlock}s is a \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}. The
\code{UnitBlock}s of the \code{UCBlock} can be given in any order the user may
wish. But to simplify the example, we assume that the
\code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is the first of the \code{UnitBlock}s (in
Figure~\ref{fig:ucblock}, it would be the \code{UnitBlock} with index
0). Hence, the description of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} should be given
in the \group \cstr{UnitBlock\_0} in the specification of the \code{UCBlock}.

The \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} (see \S \ref{sec:HydroSystemUnitBlock}) is a
\code{UnitBlock} that has a list of \code{HydroUnitBlock}s (see \S
\ref{sec:HydroUnitBlock}) and one \code{PolyhedralFunctionBlock} as nested
    {\Block}s. The \code{PolyhedralFunctionBlock} is the last among the nested
    {\Block}s of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}. Figure~\ref{fig:hsublock}
    depicts the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} with its nested {\Block}s in which
    $H$ denotes the number of \code{HydroUnitBlock}s (\dimension
    \cstr{NumberHydroUnits} in the specification of the
    \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}).

\begin{figure}[h!]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[scale=1.5]{img/hsublock.pdf}
  \caption{\code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} and its nested {\Block}s.}
  \label{fig:hsublock}
\end{figure}

The \Variables of the \code{BendersBlock} represent the dependence between
consecutive \code{UnitBlock}s. In the SSV, this dependence is related to the
volumes of the reservoirs: the initial volume of a reservoir of a
\code{HydroUnitBlock} at the outer stage $t \in \{1, \dots,
T^{\text{out}}-1\}$ must be equal to the volume of that reservoir at the last
inner stage of the \code{HydroUnitBlock} at the outer stage $t-1$.  The
\Variables of the \code{BendersBlock} at the outer stage $t \in \{0, \dots,
T^{\text{out}} - 1\}$ thus represent the initial volumes of the reservoirs of
the \code{HydroUnitBlock}s at that stage.

Let $r_i$ denote the number of reservoirs (\dimension \cstr{NumberReservoirs}
in the specification of \code{HydroUnitBlock}) of the $i$-th
\code{HydroUnitBlock}. For each $i \in \{0, \dots, H-1\}$ and $j \in
\mathcal{N}^i = \{0, \dots, r_i - 1\}$, let $\tilde{v}_{i,j}$ denote the
initial volume of the $j$-th reservoir of the $i$-th
\code{HydroUnitBlock}. These initial volumes appear in the ``final volumes
constraints'' of the \code{HydroUnitBlock}. For the inner stage $t=0$ and the
$i$-th \code{HydroUnitBlock}, these constraints are
\begin{equation}
v^{i}_{j,t} = \tilde{v}_{i,j} + F^i_{j,t} +
\Big(\sum_{l=(d,j) \in \mathcal{L}^{i}} f^i_{t -
  \tau^{i,\text{dn}}_l , l } - \sum_{l=(j,d) \in \mathcal{L}^{i} } f^i_{t -
  \tau^{i,\text{up}}_l , l }\Big), \quad \forall j \in \mathcal{N}^{i} \text{
  and } t = 0.
\label{eq:initial_volume}
\end{equation}
In these constraints, $v^{i}_{j,t}$ denotes the volume of the $j$-th reservoir
at the (inner) stage $t$, $F^i_{j,t}$ denotes the inflow at the $j$-th
reservoir at the (inner) stage $t$, $f^{i}_{k,l}$ is the flow rate at time $k$
for arc $l$, and $\tau^{i,\text{dn}}_l$ and $\tau^{i,\text{up}}_l$ denote the
uphill and downhill flow rates at arc $l$, respectively.  (See the
documentation of the \code{HydroUnitBlock} class for details.)

Therefore, we could define the \Variables $x$ of the \code{BendersBlock} as
that representing the following vector:
\begin{equation}
x =
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    anchor=base,
    label distance=10pt
  ]

\tikzset{
  s/.style={
    set fill color=blue!5,
    set border color=blue!30,
    below right offset={0.1,-0.25},
}}

\matrix [matrix of math nodes,left delimiter={[},right delimiter={]},row sep=0.1cm,column sep=0.25cm] (m) {
\tikzmarkin[s]{v0}\tilde{v}_{0,0} & \dots & \tilde{v}_{0,r_0-1}\tikzmarkend{v0} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{vk}\tilde{v}_{k,0} & \dots & \tilde{v}_{k,r_k-1}\tikzmarkend{vk} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{vH}\tilde{v}_{H-1,0} & \dots & \tilde{v}_{H-1,r_{H-1}-1}\tikzmarkend{vH}\\
};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-1)(m-1-3),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $0$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-5)(m-1-7),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $k$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(m-1-9)(m-1-11),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $H-1$
] {};
\end{tikzpicture}^{\top}.
\label{eq:benders_variables}
\end{equation}
As aforementioned, these are the active \Variables of the
\code{BendersBFunction}. Based on the values of these \Variables, the
\code{BendersBFunction} is responsible for updating the \Constraints of the
\code{HydroUnitBlock}s. By isolating the independent terms in constraints
\eqref{eq:initial_volume}, we can rewrite them as
\begin{equation}
v^{i}_{j,t} - \Big(\sum_{l=(d,j) \in \mathcal{L}^{i}}
f^i_{t - \tau^{i,\text{dn}}_l , l } - \sum_{l=(j,d)\in \mathcal{L}^{i} } f^i_{t -
  \tau^{i,\text{up}}_l , l }\Big) = \tilde{v}_{i,j} + F^i_{j,t}, \quad \forall
j \in \mathcal{N}^{i} \text{ and } t = 0.
\label{eq:initial_volume2}
\end{equation}
Notice that the independent terms appear in the right-hand side of this
constraint.  Since it is an equality constraint, the left- and right-hand
sides of the \code{RowConstraint} representing this constraint must be equal
to $\tilde{v}_{i,j} + F^i_{j,t}$ and, therefore, are identically affected by
the initial volumes of the reservoir. This means that we could define the
mapping $M$ in the \code{BendersBFunction} (see \S
\ref{sec:BendersBFunction}) as
\[
M(x) = x + F,
\]
where
\begin{equation}
F =
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    anchor=base,
    label distance=10pt
  ]

\tikzset{
  s/.style={
    set fill color=blue!5,
    set border color=blue!30,
    below right offset={0.1,-0.25},
}}

\matrix [matrix of math nodes,left delimiter={[},right delimiter={]},row sep=0.1cm,column sep=0.25cm] (F) {
\tikzmarkin[s]{F0}F^0_{0,0} & \dots & F^0_{r_0-1,0}\tikzmarkend{F0} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{Fk}F^k_{0,0} & \dots & F^k_{r_k-1,0}\tikzmarkend{Fk} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{FH}F^{H-1}_{0,0} & \dots & F^{H-1}_{r_{H-1}-1,0}\tikzmarkend{FH}\\
};

\node[
  fit=(F-1-1)(F-1-3),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $0$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(F-1-5)(F-1-7),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $k$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(F-1-9)(F-1-11),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $H-1$
] {};
\end{tikzpicture}^{\top}.
\label{eq:inflow}
\end{equation}
Since the mapping matrix $A$ is very sparse (it is the identity matrix), it
would be convenient to use the sparse representation of the mapping. The
number of rows and columns of the mapping matrix are equal to the total number
of reservoirs.  Also the number of nonzero entries in the mapping matrix is
equal to that number. Therefore, in the specification of the
\code{BendersBFunction}, we would have
\[
\cstr{NumVar} = R, \quad
\cstr{NumRow} = R, \quad \text{ and } \quad 
\cstr{NumNonzero} = R,
\]
where $R$ is the total number of reservoirs, i.e., $R = \sum_{i = 0}^{H - 1}
r_i$. Since the $A$ matrix is the identity matrix, the \variables
\cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow}, \cstr{Column}, and \cstr{A} do not need to be
provided. But just as an exercise, we explain how the $A$ matrix could be
provided in terms of the \variables \cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow}, \cstr{Column}, and
\cstr{A}. Since there is a single nonzero entry at each row, the \variable
\cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} would be a vector of size \cstr{NumRow} (i.e., $R$)
with all entries equal to 1:
\[
\cstr{NumNonzeroAtRow} = ( 1, \dots, 1 ).
\]
The \variable \cstr{Column} would be the vector
\[
\cstr{Column} = ( 0, 1, \dots, R-1 )
\]
and the \variable \cstr{A}, containing the nonzero entries of the mapping
matrix, would be the vector of size \cstr{NumNonzero} (i.e., $R$) given by
\[
\cstr{A} = ( 1, \dots, 1 ).
\]
Finally, the \variable \cstr{b}, containing the constant term of the vector, would
be the vector of size \cstr{NumRow} (i.e., $R$) given by
\[
\cstr{b} = F,
\]
with $F$ given by \eqref{eq:inflow}.

Now, to each row of the mapping, we must associate the corresponding
\code{RowConstraint}. In a \code{HydroUnitBlock},
constraints~\eqref{eq:initial_volume2} belong to a two-dimensional group of
\code{RowConstraint}s which is indexed over the dimensions (inner)
\cstr{TimeHorizon} and \cstr{NumberReservoirs}.  Thus, in that group of
\code{RowConstraint}s, the $j$-th constraint in \eqref{eq:initial_volume2} is
represented by the \code{RowConstraint} whose (two-dimensional) index is $(0,
j)$. In the specification of the \code{BendersBFunction}, each of those
\code{RowConstraint}s would be indicated by an \code{AbstractPath} starting
from the \code{BendersBlock}, i.e., having the \code{BendersBlock} as the
reference \Block.

Considering the structure of the \code{BendersBFunction} illustrated by
Figure~\ref{fig:stochastic_block} and the structure of the \code{UCBlock}
depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:ucblock}, assuming the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}
is the first \code{UnitBlock} in the \code{UCBlock}, an \code{AbstractPath}
from the \code{UCBlock} (which is the inner \Block of the
\code{BendersBFunction}) to the ``final volume'' \Constraint with
(two-dimensional) index $(0,j)$ of the $i$-th \code{HydroUnitBlock} would be
given by
\begin{equation}
  B(0) \rightarrow B(i) \rightarrow C(0, j),
  \label{path}
\end{equation}
where $B(k)$ indicated the $k$-th nested \Block of a \Block and $C(k_1, k_2)$
indicates the \Constraint whose element index is $k_2$ within the group of
\Constraints whose index is $k_1$.

To understand this path, we can start from the \RowConstraint of interest and
navigate up to the reference \Block (which is the \code{UCBlock}). Since the
two-dimensional index of the target \RowConstraint is $(0,j)$, its element
index is $j$ according to the definition of ``element index'' of a \Constraint
in an \code{AbstractPath} (see \eqref{eq:element_index} for details).
Moreover, that \RowConstraint belongs to the group of \Constraints whose index
is 0 (the ``final volume'' constraints are the first group of \Constraints
in a \code{HydroUnitBlock}; see the comments of the \code{HydroUnitBlock}
class for details). Hence, we have the node $C(0, j)$. The target
\RowConstraint belongs to the $i$-th \code{HydroUnitBlock}, i.e., the $i$-th
nested \Block of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}. Hence, we have the node
$B(i)$. Finally, the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is the first nested \Block of
the \code{UCBlock}. Hence, we have the node $B(0)$.

The \code{AbstractPath}s of those \RowConstraints must be given as a vector of
\code{AbstractPath}. From the general path \eqref{path} to a \RowConstraint,
it is straightforward to construct a vector of \code{AbstractPath}s to those
\RowConstraints. By concatenating the sequence of nodes of all
\code{AbstractPath}s, we would have the following vectors of size $3R$:
\begin{align*}
\cstr{PathNodeTypes} = ( & \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`C'}, \ \dots, \
\text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`C'})\hspace{8.2cm}\\
%\cstr{PathGroupIndices} = ( & 0, 0, 0, \dots, 0, 0, 0, \quad 0, 1, 0, \dots, 0, 1, 0, \quad \dots, \quad
%0, H-1, 0, \dots, 0, H-1, 0)\\
%
\cstr{PathElementIndices} = (
& \code{inf}, \code{inf}, 0, \ \dots, \ \code{inf}, \code{inf}, r_0-1,\\
& \code{inf}, \code{inf}, 0, \ \dots, \ \code{inf}, \code{inf}, r_1-1,\\
& \dots,\\
& \code{inf}, \code{inf}, 0, \ \dots, \ \code{inf}, \code{inf}, r_{H-1}-1 )
\end{align*}
\[
\cstr{PathGroupIndices} = (
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    anchor=base,
    label distance=10pt,
  ]
\tikzset{
  s/.style={
    set fill color=blue!0,
    set border color=blue!0,
}}
\matrix [matrix of math nodes, row sep=0.1cm, column sep=0.1cm, outer ysep=0mm, inner ysep=0mm,] (P) {
  \tikzmarkin[s]{P0}0, 0, 0, \ \dots, \ 0, 0, 0,\tikzmarkend{P0} & &
  \tikzmarkin[s]{Pk}0, 1, 0, \ \dots, \ 0, 1, 0,\tikzmarkend{Pk} & \dots, &
  \tikzmarkin[s]{PH}0, H-1, 0, \ \dots, \ 0, H-1, 0\tikzmarkend{PH}\\
};
\node[
  fit=(P-1-1)(P-1-1),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:$r_0$ times
] {};
\node[
  fit=(P-1-3)(P-1-3),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:$r_1$ times
] {};
\node[
  fit=(P-1-5)(P-1-5),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:$r_{H-1}$ times
] {};
\end{tikzpicture} ).
\]
Notice how \cstr{PathGroupIndices} is constructed. It is formed by $H-1$
sub-vectors. The $i$-th sub-vector is associated with the $i$-th
\code{HydroUnitBlock}, has size $3r_i$, and is formed by concatenating
$(0,i,0)$ $r_i$ times. Since each \code{AbstractPath} has three nodes and
there are a total of $R$ \code{AbstractPath}s, we would have
\[
\cstr{PathDim} = R, \quad \cstr{TotalLength} = 3R, \quad \text{ and } \quad
\cstr{PathStart} = (0, 3, 6, \dots, 3(R - 1)).
\]

Lastly, since all those \RowConstraints represent equality constraints, the
\cstr{ConstraintSide} \variable would be a vector of size \cstr{NumRow} (i.e.,
$R$) with all elements equal to `B', meaning that both sides of the
\RowConstraint are equally affected:
\[
\cstr{ConstraintSide} = (\text{`B'}, \dots, \text{`B'}).
\]
But since all elements are equal to `B', the \cstr{ConstraintSide} \variable
does not need to be provided.

\subsubsection{\code{StochasticBlock}}\label{sec:StochasticBlock_example}

The \group \cstr{Block} of the \cstr{StochasticBlock} must contain the
description of the associated \code{BendersBlock}. Moreover, the
\cstr{StochasticBlock} also requires a vector of \code{SimpleDataMapping}
which, in the case of the SSV, defines how the data of the \code{UCBlock}
is updated considering a given sub-scenario. A sub-scenario is a vector of
\code{double} containing a realization of the stochastic data of the
\code{UCBlock}. Each \code{SimpleDataMapping} (see \S
\ref{sec:SimpleDataMapping}) would be associated with a particular part of the
sub-scenario and a particular stochastic data of some \dBlock. A
\code{SimpleDataMapping} requires five elements:
\begin{itemize}
\item A \dBlock whose data is affected.

\item The name of the function that will be responsible for updating the data
  of that \dBlock.

\item An ordered multiset of indices that specifies which elements of the
  sub-scenario should be considered. This multiset must therefore contain
  elements between 0 and $n-1$, where $n$ is the size of the sub-scenario.  We
  call this multiset the \code{SetFrom} multiset.

\item An ordered multiset of indices that specifies which elements of the
  \dBlock will be updated. We call this multiset the \code{SetTo} multiset.

\item The type of the data that will be updated.
\end{itemize}
(A multiset is a set that can contain multiple instances of the same
element. For instance, $\{2, 2, 4, 4, 7\}$ is a multiset with cardinality
5. An ordered multiset is a multiset in which the order of the elements
matter. For instance, $\{2, 2, 4, 4, 7\}$ and $\{2, 4, 4, 2, 7\}$ are
different multisets. In most cases, if not all, the \code{SetFrom} and
\code{SetTo} multisets will be simple sets.) The \code{SetFrom} multiset
defines a mapping $\mathcal{M}: \mathbb{R}^n \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^m$, where
$n$ is the size of the sub-scenario and $m$ is the cardinality of the
\code{SetFrom} multiset. Let $s_i$ denote the $i$-th element of the
\code{SetFrom} multiset. For a given sub-scenario $\xi$, $\mathcal{M}(\xi)$ is
the vector whose $i$-th element is $\xi_{s_i}$. The \code{SetTo} multiset will
be used to associate each element of $\mathcal{M}(\xi)$ to a particular
element of in the data structure of the \dBlock.

Consider, for example, a \code{UCBlock} that has $N$ \code{UnitBlock}s, one of
them being a \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} and two of them being
\code{ThermalUnitBlock}s. Suppose that the maximum power of the first
\code{ThermalUnitBlock} is stochastic for the inner stages 2, 3, and 5 and
that the maximum power of the second \code{ThermalUnitBlock} is stochastic for
the inner stages 0, 1, 2, and 3. Suppose also that the \code{NetworkBlock}
associated with the inner stage 1 has 10 nodes, indexed from 0 to 9, and that
the active demand at the node with index 4 is stochastic. Finally, suppose
that the fourth \code{HydroUnitBlock} of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} has
some stochastic inflows. The inflow occurs at each reservoir of a
\code{HydroUnitBlock} at each inner stage. To simplify, let us suppose that
the inflows at the second reservoir at the inner stage 0 and the inflows at
the fifth reservoir at the inner stage 15 are stochastic.

We could define a sub-scenario $\xi$ as a vector in $\mathbb{R}^{10}$ such
that
\begin{itemize}
\item $(\xi_0, \xi_1, \xi_2)$ represent the maximum power of the first
  \code{ThermalUnitBlock} for the inner stages 2, 3, and 5.
\item $(\xi_3, \xi_4, \xi_5, \xi_6)$ represent the maximum power of the second
  \code{ThermalUnitBlock} for the inner stages 0, 1, 2, and 3.
\item $(\xi_7)$ represent the active demand of the \code{NetworkBlock}
  associated with the inner stage 1 at node with index 4.
\item $(\xi_8, \xi_9)$ represent the inflows at the second and fifth
  reservoirs of the fourth \code{HydroUnitBlock} at the inner stages 0 and
  15, respectively.
\end{itemize}

In this case, we can construct four \code{SimpleDataMapping}s to update the
stochastic data of these \Blocks and one \code{SimpleDataMapping} to update
the \code{BendersBFunction} (which depends on the inflow at the first inner
stage). First of all, here is a list of functions that are registered in the
methods factory. In the context of the SSV, they can be used to define the
stochastic data (through the \code{SimpleDataMapping}) as we will see in the
sequel.
\begin{itemize}
\item \code{DCNetworkBlock::set\_active\_demand}\\ This function updates the
  active demand of a \code{NetworkBlock}. The active demand is represented by
  a one-dimensional array whose $i$-th element is the active demand at the
  $i$-th node.

\item \code{ThermalUnitBlock::set\_maximum\_power}\\ This function updates the
  maximum active power output of a \code{ThermalUnitBlock}. The maximum active
  power output is represented by a one-dimensional array whose $t$-th element
  is the maximum active power output at the inner stage $t$.

\item \code{HydroUnitBlock::set\_inflow}\\ This function updates the inflow of
  a \code{HydroUnitBlock}. The inflow is represented by a two-dimensional
  array whose element at position $(i, t)$ is the inflow at node $i$ at the
  inner stage $t$.

\item \code{IntermittentUnitBlock::set\_maximum\_power}\\ This function
  updates the maximum potential power production of a
  \code{IntermittentUnitBlock}. The maximum potential power production is
  represented by a one-dimensional array whose $t$-th element is the maximum
  potential power production at the inner stage $t$.

\item \code{BendersBFunction::modify\_constants}\\ This function updates the
  constant $b$ of the mapping of the \code{BendersBFunction} (see
  \eqref{eq:benders_mapping} in \S \ref{sec:BendersBFunction}).
\end{itemize}

Let us begin with the first \code{ThermalUnitBlock}. Its data is given in the
first two entries of the sub-scenario and is associated with the maximum power
at the inner stages 2, 3, and 5. Therefore, we have $\{0,1,2\}$ as the
\code{SetFrom} set and $\{2,3,5\}$ as the \code{SetTo} set. Since the maximum
power is of type \code{double}, its data type is `D'. The name of the function
responsible for updating the maximum power is
\code{ThermalUnitBlock::set\_maximum\_power}. Finally, we need to specify that
this data is associated with our first \code{ThermalUnitBlock}. This is done
by providing an \code{AbstractPath} to that \Block. Suppose that this
\code{ThermalUnitBlock} is the second sub-\Block of the \code{UCBlock} so that
its group index is 1 (this \code{ThermalUnitBlock} would be the
\code{UnitBlock} with index 1 in Figure~\ref{fig:ucblock} and associated with
\group \code{UnitBlock\_0} in the specification of the \code{UCBlock}; see \S
\ref{sec:UCBlock}). The path from the \code{BendersBlock} to this
\code{ThermalUnitBlock} would then be given by
\[
  O \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(1).
\]
Analogously, for the second \code{ThermalUnitBlock}, we would have
$\{3,4,5,6\}$ as the \code{SetFrom} set, $\{0,1,2,3\}$ as the
\code{SetTo} set and, assuming it is the fourth sub-\Block of the
\code{UCBlock} (and thus its group index is 5), the path to this \Block would
be
\[
  O \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(5).
\]
For the \code{NetworkBlock}, we would have $\{7\}$ as the \code{SetFrom}
set and $\{4\}$ (the index of the node) as the \code{SetTo}
set. The name of the function that updates this data is
\code{DCNetworkBlock::set\_active\_demand} and the data is also of type
\code{double} (indicated by `D'). Finally, we need to specify the path from
the \code{BendersBlock} to this \code{NetworkBlock}. As we see in
Figure~\ref{fig:ucblock}, the \code{NetworkBlock}s come after the
\code{UnitBlock}s. Since in our example the \code{UCBlock} has $N$
\code{UnitBlock}s and the \code{NetworkBlock} of interest is associated with
stage 1 (the stages being indexed from 0 to $T^{\text{in}}$), the group index
of this \code{NetworkBlock} would be $N+1$. Therefore, the path from the
\code{BendersBlock} to this \code{NetworkBlock} would be given by
\[
  O \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(N+1).
\]
For the \code{HydroUnitBlock}, we would have $\{8,9\}$ as the \code{SetFrom}.
Since the inflow of a \code{HydroUnitBlock} is associated with a
two-dimensional data structure, the specification of the \code{SetTo} is a
little bit more complicated (but not too much). This data structure -- let us
call it \code{inflow} -- is such that \code{inflow[i][t]} gives the inflow at
reservoir $i$ at the inner stage $t$. As you may notice, a single inflow is
identified by two indices (the index of the reservoir and the inner stage)
while \code{SetTo} is a set of simple indices. So, we must convert a pair of
indices into a single index. The conversion is made considering that a
multi-dimensional structure is vectorized by making its last dimension vary
faster. This means that for a two-dimensional matrix $A$ with $m$ rows $a_0,
\dots, a_{m-1}$ and $n$ columns, its vectorized form is given by arranging its
rows in sequence: $( a_0 , a_1 , \dots, a_{m-1} )$. Then, the element at
position $(i,j)$ in the matrix $A$ is the element at position $i \times n + j$
in its vector representation. See~\eqref{eq:element_index} for the index of an
element in an arbitrarily multi-dimensional array. The pairs of indices that
we need to convert are $(1,0)$ (the second reservoir, whose index is 1, and
the inner stage 0) and $(4,15)$ (the fifth reservoir, whose index is 4, and
the inner stage 15). Since the fourth \code{HydroUnitBlock} has $r_3$
reservoirs and the inner time horizon is $T^{\text{in}}$, the data structure
representing the inflows is a $r_3 \times T^{\text{in}}$ two-dimensional
array. Thus, the single indices associated with $(1,0)$ and $(4,15)$ are
$T^{\text{in}}$ and $4T^{\text{in}} + 15$, respectively. Thus, we would have
$\{T^{\text{in}}, 4T^{\text{in}} + 15\}$ as the \code{SetTo} set. The name of
the function that updates the inflow is \code{HydroUnitBlock::set\_inflow} and
the data is of type \code{double} (indicated by `D'). Finally, we need to
provide the path from the \code{BendersBlock} to this
\code{HydroUnitBlock}. We are assuming that the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is
the first \Block in the \code{UCBlock}. Therefore, since our
\code{HydroUnitBlock} of interest has index 3 (it is the fourth
\code{HydroUnitBlock} of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}), the path would be
given by
\[
  O \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(3).
\]
As we saw in \S \ref{sec:ssv_benders_block}, the mapping of the
\code{BendersBFunction} depends on the inflows at the first inner stage. In
our example, the inflow at the second reservoir of the fourth
\code{HydroUnitBlock} at the inner stage 0 is stochastic. Therefore, we also
need a \code{SimpleDataMapping} to update the mapping of the
\code{BendersBFunction}. In that mapping, the inflows appear in the vector $F$
defined in \eqref{eq:inflow}. In $F$, the initial inflow at the second
reservoir of the fourth \code{HydroUnitBlock} is linked with index $r_0 + r_1
+ r_2 + 1$. Thus, in the \code{SimpleDataMapping} used to update the
\code{BendersBFunction} we would have $\{8\}$ as the \code{SetFrom} set (since
the initial inflow at the second reservoir of the fourth \code{HydroUnitBlock}
is given by $\xi_8$) and $\{r_0 + r_1 + r_2 + 1\}$ as the \code{SetTo}
set. The name of the function that updates the constant part of the mapping of
the \code{BendersBFunction} is \code{BendersBFunction::modify\_constants} and
the data is of type \code{double} (indicated by `D'). The path from the
\code{BendersBlock} to the \code{BendersBFunction} is given by
\[
O.
\]
In the specification of the \code{StochasticBlock}, these
\code{SimpleDataMapping}s must be provided as a vector of
\code{SimpleDataMapping} (see \S \ref{sec:vector_SimpleDataMapping}). Since
there are five \code{SimpleDataMapping}s, we have $\cstr{NumberDataMappings}
= 5$. All stochastic data have a type \code{double}, so \cstr{DataType} does
not need to be provided. Anyway, it could be specified as
\begin{center}
\cstr{DataType} = (`D', `D', `D', `D', `D').
\end{center}
All multisets can be provided as \code{Subset}s. However, since the
representation of a \code{Range} is more compact for sets with at least three
elements, we will use it whenever is convenient. Table~\ref{tab:sets} shows
the sets that we need to provide.
\begin{table}
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
    \hline
    \code{SetFrom} & \code{SetTo}\\
    \hline
    $\{0,1,2\}$ & $\{2,3,5\}$\\
    $\{3,4,5,6\}$ & $\{0,1,2,3\}$\\
    $\{7\}$ & $\{4\}$\\
    $\{8,9\}$ & $\{T^{\text{in}}, 4T^{\text{in}} + 15\}$\\
    $\{8\}$ & $\{r_0 + r_1 + r_2 + 1\}$\\
    \hline
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{The \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets.}
  \label{tab:sets}
\end{table}
The first two \code{SetFrom} sets and the second \code{SetTo} set have at
least three elements, so we will use a \code{Range} to represent each of
them. We begin with the \code{SetSize} \variable. This one-dimensional
\variable indicates the size or the type of each set. For a \code{Subset}, we
must include its size in \code{SetSize}. For a \code{Range}, we must include
the number 0 in \code{SetSize}. Thus, in our example, this \variable would be
given as
\[
\code{SetSize} = ( 0, 3, \quad 0, 0, \quad 1, 1, \quad 2, 2, \quad 1, 1).
\]
Recall that the elements at positions $2i$ and $2i+1$ correspond to the sizes
(or types) of the $i$-th \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets. Now, we must
specify the elements that belong to each set. For a \code{Range} representing
the set $[a,b)$, we must provide $a$ and $b$. For a \code{Subset}, we must
provide its elements. Thus, the \cstr{SetElements} \variable would be given as
\[
\cstr{SetElements} = ( 0, 3, 2, 3, 5, \quad 3, 7, 0, 4, \quad 7, 4, \quad
8, 9, T^{\text{in}}, 4T^{\text{in}} + 15, \quad 8, r_0 + r_1 + r_2 + 1).
\]
(The first \code{SimpleDataMapping} has $\{0, 1, 2\}$ as its \code{SetFrom}
set and $\{2,3,5\}$ as its \code{SetTo} set. In this case, the \code{SetFrom}
can be represented as the \code{Range} $[0, 3)$. The \code{SetTo} cannot be
represented as a \code{Range} (it is not an interval of integers), so we
need to explicitly list all its elements. Thus, the first elements of
\code{SetSize} are 0 and 3 (indicating that the first \code{SetFrom} set is a
\code{Range} and the first \code{SetTo} set is a \code{Subset} with three
elements). Moreover, the first elements of \code{SetElements} are 0 and 3
(describing the first \code{SetFrom}) and 2, 3, and 5 (describing the first
\code{SetTo}). The next elements of \code{SetSize} and \code{SetElements} are
those describing the \code{SetFrom} and \code{SetTo} sets of the second
\code{SimpleDataMapping}. In the second \code{SimpleDataMapping}, we have
$\{3,4,5,6\}$ as the \code{SetFrom} set and $\{0,1,2,3\}$ as the \code{SetTo}
set. These sets can be represented as the \code{Range}s $[3, 7)$ and $[0, 4)$,
respectively. So, the next elements of \code{SetSize} are 0, 0 (indicating
that both sets are \code{Range}s) and the next elements of
\code{SetElements} are 3, 7, 0, 4. And so on.)

The \cstr{FunctionName} \variable must contain the names of the functions
(registered in the ``methods factory'') responsible for updating the data of
the \Blocks. In our example, we would have
\begin{align*}
  \cstr{FunctionName} = (
  & ``\code{ThermalUnitBlock::set\_maximum\_power}" ,\\
  & ``\code{ThermalUnitBlock::set\_maximum\_power}" ,\\
  & ``\code{DCNetworkBlock::set\_active\_demand}" ,\\
  & ``\code{HydroUnitBlock::set\_inflow}" ,\\
  & ``\code{BendersBFunction::modify\_constants}" ).
\end{align*}

We must also provide a vector of \code{AbstractPath} to the affected \Blocks
and the \code{BendersBFunction}. Since we have five \code{SimpleDataMapping}s,
we also have five paths. So, we have $\cstr{PathDim} = 5$. In our example, the
first three paths have size 3, the fourth path has size 4, and the last path
has size 1. So, we have
\[
\cstr{TotalLength} = 14 \quad \text{ and } \quad \cstr{PathStart} = (0, 3, 6, 9, 13).
\]
We also have
\begin{align*}
\cstr{PathNodeTypes} = ( &
  \text{`O'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \quad
  \text{`O'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \quad
  \text{`O'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \quad
  \text{`O'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \quad
  \text{`O'})\\
\cstr{PathGroupIndices} = ( &
  \code{inf}, 0, 1, \quad
  \code{inf}, 0, 5, \quad
  \code{inf}, 0, N+1, \quad
  \code{inf}, 0, 1, 3, \quad
  \code{inf})\\
\cstr{PathElementIndices} = ( &
  \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \quad
  \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \quad
  \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \quad
  \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \quad
  \code{inf}).
\end{align*}
Since all elements of \cstr{PathElementIndices} are equal to \code{inf}, this
\variable does not need to be provided. Finally, we need to inform the type of
each affected object. The first four objects are \Blocks while the last one is
a \Function. Thus, we have
\[
\cstr{Caller} = (\text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`F'}).
\]

\subsubsection{\code{SDDPBlock}}

We now describe the remaining part of the \code{SDDPBlock}. In the beginning
of \S \ref{sec:SSV}, we have seen how the scenarios are specified in the
\code{SDDPBlock}. According to our notation, the \dimension \cstr{TimeHorizon}
of \code{SDDPBlock} would be equal to $T^{\text{out}}$. Following the example
of \S \ref{sec:StochasticBlock_example}, each sub-scenario (a part of the
scenario that is associated with a particular outer stage) has size
10. Assuming all sub-scenarios have the same size, there would be no need to
provide the \dimension \cstr{SubScenarioSize} and the \dimension
\cstr{ScenarioSize} would be equal to $10 T^{\text{out}}$ (for each stage $t
\in \{0, \dots, T^{\text{out}}-1\}$, the size of a sub-scenario associated
with stage $t$ would be 10). Also, assuming there is a total of $m$ scenarios,
the \dimension \cstr{NumberScenarios} would be equal to $m$. In our example,
we can identify three groups of related random data: maximum power of a
\code{ThermalUnitBlock}, active demand of a \code{NetworkBlock}, and inflows
of a \code{HydroUnitBlock}. Thus, the \dimension \cstr{NumberRandomDataGroups}
would be equal to 3. The first group would then have size 6 (the maximum power
of both affected \code{ThermalUnitBlock}s) and the second and third groups
would have each one size 2. Therefore, the \cstr{SizeRandomDataGroups}
\variable would be given by
\[
\cstr{SizeRandomDataGroups} = (6, 2, 2).
\]
For instance, if we had $T^{\text{out}}=3$ and $m=5$, the \cstr{Scenarios}
\variable could be the following matrix:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    label distance=10pt
  ]
\tikzset{
  sin/.style={
    above left offset={-0.2,0.45},
    below right offset={0.2,-0.3},
    set fill color=gray!10,
    set border color=gray!40,
  },
  sin1/.style={
    set fill color=green!5,
    set border color=green!30,
  },
  sin2/.style={
    set fill color=red!5,
    set border color=red!30,
  },
  sin3/.style={
    set fill color=blue!5,
    set border color=blue!30,
}}

\matrix [matrix of math nodes,left delimiter={[},right delimiter={]},row sep=0.5cm,column sep=0.1cm] (me) {

] {};

inner xsep=0,
above delimiter=\{,
label=above:Stage $2$
] {};

fit=(me-1-32.center),
inner xsep=5pt,inner ysep=0,
label=right:Scenario $0$,
] {};
\node[
fit=(me-2-32.center),
inner xsep=5pt,inner ysep=0,
label=right:Scenario $1$,
] {};
\node[
fit=(me-3-32.center),
inner xsep=5pt,inner ysep=0,
label=right:Scenario $2$,
] {};

\end{equation*}
The green, red, and blue boxes indicate the maximum power of the
\code{ThermalUnitBlock}s, the active demand of the \code{NetworkBlock}, and
the inflows of the \code{HydroUnitBlock}, respectively. The gray box indicates
a sub-scenario.

The last sub-\Block of a \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is a
\code{PolyhedralFunctionBlock} (see \S \ref{sec:PolyhedralFunctionBlock})
which contains a \code{PolyhedralFunction} (see \S
\ref{sec:PolyhedralFunction}). For each $t \in \{0, \dots,
T^{\text{out}}-2\}$, the \code{PolyhedralFunction} associated with the outer
stage $t$ represents an approximation to the (unknown) expected future cost
function. This is the function that is updated at each step of the SDDP
method. This function depends on the volumes of all reservoirs at the last
inner stage ($T^{\text{in}}-1$). Following the same notation used to describe
constraints \eqref{eq:initial_volume2}, this function depends on the variables
$v^i_{j,T^{\text{in}}-1}$ for each \code{HydroUnitBlock} $i \in \{0, \dots,
H-1\}$ and for each reservoir $j \in \mathcal{N}^i$ of that
\code{HydroUnitBlock}. The only thing that must be provided in the
specification of the \code{PolyhedralFunction} is the \dimension
\cstr{PolyFunction\_NumVar}. This \dimension must be equal to the total number
of reservoirs (which is $R$ in our example).

If one wishes to provide an initial approximation of the future cost function,
then other \variables and \dimensions must also be provided. The
\code{PolyhedralFunction} represents a function $f$ of the form
\begin{equation}
  f(x) = \max_{k \in \{0,\dots,p-1\}} a_k^\top x + b_k
  \label{eq:poly_max}
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
  f(x) = \min_{k \in \{0,\dots,p-1\}} a_k^\top x + b_k.
  \label{eq:poly_min}
\end{equation}
The \dimension \cstr{PolyFunction\_sign} indicates whether the
\code{PolyhedralFunction} has the form \eqref{eq:poly_max}, and therefore is a
convex, or \eqref{eq:poly_min}, and thus is concave. In the SSV, the future
cost function is convex and has the form \eqref{eq:poly_max}, so that
\cstr{PolyFunction\_sign} should be \code{true}. The \dimension
\cstr{PolyFunction\_NumRow} indicates the number of affine functions that
define the polyhedral function. In \eqref{eq:poly_max} and
\eqref{eq:poly_min}, this number is $p$. The vectors $a_k$ and the constants
$b_k$ are provided in the \cstr{PolyFunction\_A} and \cstr{PolyFunction\_b}
\variables, respectively. \cstr{PolyFunction\_A} must contain a matrix $A \in
\mathbb{R}^{p \times R}$ such that, for each $k \in \{0, \dots, p-1\}$, $a_k$
is its $k$-th row, and \cstr{PolyFunction\_b} is a one-dimensional array whose
$k$-th element is $b_k$. Finally, it is also possible to indicate a global
lower (or upper) bound on the value of the function by setting the
\cstr{PolyFunction\_lb} \variable. In the SSV, a valid global lower bound on
the value of the future cost function is zero.

In the specification of the \code{SDDPBlock}, it is necessary to specify a
path to each of these \code{PolyhedralFunction}s. For each outer stage $t \in
\{0, \dots, T^{\text{out}}-2\}$, the required path is an \code{AbstracPath}
from the \code{BendersBlock} associated with stage $t$ to the
\code{PolyhedralFunction}. Recall that we assumed that the
\code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is the first sub-\Block of the \code{UCBlock}, so
that its group index is 0. Thus, the path from the \code{BendersBlock} to the
\code{PolyhedralFunction} would be the following:
\begin{equation}
  O \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(0) \rightarrow B(H).
  \label{path_poly}
\end{equation}
We have seen in \S \ref{sec:StochasticBlock_example} an example on how to
specify a vector of \code{AbstractPath}s. Here we assume that all paths are
equal (that is, we assume that the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock} is the first
sub-\Block of \code{UCBlock} and that the \code{PolyhedralFunctionBlock} is
the last sub-\Block of the \code{HydroSystemUnitBlock}). In this case, a
vector with a single \code{AbstractPath} can be provided. Thus, the
\cstr{PathDim} \dimension would be 1 and, according \eqref{path_poly}, we
would have \cstr{TotalLength} = 4, \cstr{PathStart} = (0) and
\begin{align*}
\cstr{PathNodeTypes} = ( &
\text{`O'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'}, \text{`B'})\\
\cstr{PathGroupIndices} = ( &
  \code{inf}, 0, 0, H)\\
\cstr{PathElementIndices} = ( &
  \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}, \code{inf}).
\end{align*}
Again, since all elements of \cstr{PathElementIndices} are equal to
\code{inf}, this \variable does not need to be provided.

The \code{SDDPBlock} also requires an admissible state for each outer
stage. In the SSV, a state for a given stage is formed by the initial volumes
of all the reservoirs at that stage. Thus, the \cstr{StateSize} would be equal
to $R$, the total number of reservoirs. An admissible state for stage $t \in
\{0, \dots, T^{\text{out}}-1\}$ is a state for which the sub-problem at stage
$t$ is feasible. Trivial examples of non-admissible states include those in
which the volumes of the reservoirs are too low (so that the demand may not be
satisfied) or too high (so that the maximum volume limit of the reservoir may
be exceeded if there is enough inflow). The state is a vector that must follow
the same convention as that chosen for the vector $x$ defined in
\eqref{eq:benders_variables}. So, a state $s^t$ for the outer stage $t \in
\{0, \dots, T^{\text{out}}-1\}$ must be a vector in $\mathbb{R}^R$ with the
form
\begin{equation}
s^t =
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    baseline,
    anchor=base,
    label distance=10pt
  ]

\tikzset{
  s/.style={
    set fill color=blue!5,
    set border color=blue!30,
    below right offset={0.1,-0.25},
}}

\matrix [matrix of math nodes,left delimiter={[},right delimiter={]},row sep=0.1cm,column sep=0.25cm] (mstate) {
\tikzmarkin[s]{state0}s^t_{0,0} & \dots & s^t_{0,r_0}\tikzmarkend{state0} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{statek}s^t_{i,0} & \dots & s^t_{i,r_i}\tikzmarkend{statek} & \dots &
\tikzmarkin[s]{stateH}s^t_{H-1,0} & \dots & s^t_{H-1,r_{H-1}}\tikzmarkend{stateH}\\
};

\node[
  fit=(mstate-1-1)(mstate-1-3),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $0$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(mstate-1-5)(mstate-1-7),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $i$
] {};

\node[
  fit=(mstate-1-9)(mstate-1-11),
  inner xsep=0,
  below delimiter=\},
  label=below:\code{HydroUnitBlock} $H-1$
] {};
\end{tikzpicture}
\label{eq:state}
\end{equation}
where $s^t_{i,j}$ is the initial volume for the $j$-th reservoir of the $i$-th
\code{HydroUnitBlock}. The \cstr{AdmissibleState} \variable would then be a
one-dimensional array of size $RT^{\text{out}}$ containing the states for all
outer stages arranged as follows:
\[
\cstr{AdmissibleState} = ( s^0, \dots, s^{T^{\text{out}}-1}),
\]
where $s^t \in \mathbb{R}^R$ is given by \eqref{eq:state} for each $t \in \{0,
\dots, T^{\text{out}}-1\}$.

%- - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -
\section{Conclusion}

This document is intended as a quick reference manual for anybody who is required to construct \SMSpp \netCDF input files. It should be remarked that basically all the \dBlock and \dConfiguration objects are supposed to have an in-memory interface to initialize the data. Thus, a workable way to produce \netCDF input files for those is to use the in-memory interface, and then the \code{serialize()} method to save it into a file. Yet, \netCDF has interfaces for all primary programming languages, hence files with the required format can be written independently by any \SMSpp object.
